## management-governance — module synthesis

_Source: `99_management-governance-synthesis.md`_

# Management-Governance Module — DHER (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Delivery Hero's stewardship record does not clear the bar for a trusted steward of shareholder capital: a founder-CEO with fifteen years of continuity has hit his own guided Adjusted EBITDA target three years running, but that operating discipline sits on top of a capital-allocation record that destroyed per-share value — €2,054m of cumulative goodwill impairment against two debt-and-stock-funded mega-deals (Woowa, Glovo), a 49.6% five-year share-count increase with zero buybacks, and book value per share down 75.4%. Incentives are only partly aligned — a genuine FY2024 redesign put 70% of the long-term plan on relative total shareholder return, but a large legacy option pool rewarded pure revenue growth through years of billion-euro losses, and insiders own just 0.38% of the company, entirely compensation-derived. The single biggest governance risk is an auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain (a €440–770m contingent liability) that hard-locks this verdict regardless of the operating record, compounded by a live Uber tender offer in which management's equity fully vests on a change of control — a personal incentive detached from the price other shareholders receive. Verdict: serious governance concerns.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict:** Serious governance concerns
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** Y — verbatim: "KPMG AG's Independent Auditor's Report carries a dedicated section titled 'Material Uncertainty about the Ability of Subsidiaries to Continue as a Going Concern,' tied to Glovoapp Spain Platform S.L.U. and the ongoing Spanish social-security rider-classification investigation (contingent liability disclosed at €440–770 million). [FY24 Annual Report, Independent Auditor's Report, p.222–223] The report explicitly states the group-level opinions 'have not been modified in this regard,' so this is a subsidiary-level material-uncertainty (going-concern) paragraph inside an otherwise unmodified/unqualified opinion — not a qualified opinion and not a going-concern doubt over the whole Group." [`business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, Section 2]
- Management quality /100: **60** (capped per §24 Filter 2 / RF-MGT-004 — turnaround narrative proven on Adjusted EBITDA for 3 years, not yet proven on statutory operating profit, which has only 1 clean year — `01_management-and-track-record.md`)
- Capital allocation /100: **40** (`02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`; Weak band; independently below the RF-CAP-004 cap of 50)
- Incentive alignment /100: **57** (`03_incentives-and-compensation.md`; Mixed band)
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **60** (capped per partial-data rule — no full beneficial-ownership table, no PDMR transaction log; raw pre-cap total from `05` was 67 — `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`)
- Disclosure candor /100: **53** (`06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`; Mixed band)
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse)*: **80** (floor applied per Disqualifier Deference — the hard disqualifier's floor of ≥80 is more restrictive than the RF-CAP-004 serial-acquirer floor of ≥60, so the higher floor governs)
- Data quality /100: **78** (from `00` — Sufficient verdict; proxy-equivalent, comp, board, and RPT disclosure all present and mostly Tier-5 sourced; capped down from higher by the ownership/PDMR gap and the ~15.6-month-old audited filing)
- Overall usefulness /100: **85** (no proxy/comp-disclosure cap applies since compensation disclosure is present and detailed; this module surfaces the disqualifier context, the M&A value-destruction record, the change-of-control incentive conflict, and the forward squeeze-out risk in enough depth to drive the master synthesizer's verdict)
- Insider ownership (one line): Management Board holds **0.38%** of subscribed capital (1,098,191 / 287,385,940 shares, Dec-31-2024), entirely compensation-derived (LTIP/STI/Hero Grant equity) with no evidenced open-market purchase; no pledging disclosed [`04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`, Finding 04-002/04-003/04-005]
- Biggest governance signal (one line): A subsidiary-level auditor going-concern note (Glovo Spain, up to €770m contingent liability) sits alongside a live Uber tender offer in which the Management Board's unvested equity fully accelerates on a change-of-control resignation — a personal incentive independent of the per-share price other shareholders receive
- **Governance Score /100** — `0.20×40 + 0.18×57 + 0.18×60 + 0.16×53 + 0.16×60 + 0.12×(100−80)` = `8.00 + 10.26 + 10.80 + 8.48 + 9.60 + 2.40` = **50**
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100** (= 50 × 72/100): **36**
- **Governance Rating:** Weak (Governance Score 50 falls in the 40–54 band; independently confirmed by the Disqualifier Deference rule, which forces the rating no better than "Weak")
- **Confidence Score /100** (source quality): **72** — most core governance disclosures (comp structure, board composition, RPT, capital-allocation history) are Tier-5 (KPMG-audited Annual Report), but several current, materially relevant facts (Woowa/Glovo original deal prices, Prosus/Aspex 2026 stake changes, CEO succession reporting, CFO's pre-DHER career) rest on unverified web sources, and no FY2025 audited Annual Report is yet in the pool
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** 8 / 0 *(this module's own Red-Flag Trigger Engine found zero locally-originating Critical-severity flags; the one Critical-severity item in this dossier — the going-concern note — is a hard disqualifier owned and already adjudicated by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`, applied here via the Disqualifier Deference rule rather than re-counted in this module's own register)

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** Capital allocation destroyed per-share value on the record, not on inference — €2,054m of cumulative goodwill impairment FY2021–FY2025 against two debt-and-stock-funded mega-deals (Woowa ~$4.0bn, Glovo €2.3bn), book value per share down 75.4% (€22.32→€5.50), tangible book value per share negative in every year of the window, and share count up 49.6% with zero buybacks in any year — the clearest evidence that per-share value was not the organizing principle of the last five years [`02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`, Findings 02-009, 02-020, 02-021].
- **Strongest bull point (steelman):** The FY2024 incentive redesign is a real, cited improvement — 70% of the Long-Term Incentive now weights relative Total Shareholder Return (a genuine per-share, market-tested metric), malus/clawback was extended to cover both STI and LTI for the first time, and management has hit its own guided Adjusted EBITDA target within 1–2 percentage points in each of FY2023, FY2024, and FY2025 — a real, checkable three-year execution record on the metric it controls [`03_incentives-and-compensation.md`, Findings 03-007, 03-012; `01_management-and-track-record.md`, Findings 01-008–01-010].
- **Single killer risk (stewardship-specific):** The live Uber tender offer, layered on a change-of-control provision that fully and unconditionally vests all Management Board members' unvested STI/LTI on resignation within three months of a change of control — a direct personal financial incentive to see the deal close, independent of the per-share price achieved for other shareholders — combined with Uber's own statement that it will "evaluate the most appropriate ownership structure" once its economic ownership exceeds 50%, which raises real post-closing squeeze-out/domination-agreement risk for any minority holder who does not tender [`03_incentives-and-compensation.md`, Finding 03-018; `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`, Finding 05-021].
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** Management answered pointed, direct analyst questions candidly when challenged (the FCF-exclusion mechanics, the Glovo Spain contingent-liability range, and the "it's obviously a clear problem for us... it's not our decision" answer on the Uber overhang) rather than evading [`06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`, Findings 06-008, 06-010]; say-on-pay support was 93.05%; there is no dual-class structure, no promoter/insider pledging, and related-party transactions sit at ≈0.2% of revenue, immaterial and individually disclosed [`04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`, Finding 04-005; `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`, Findings 05-010, 05-013].

## 2. Specialist Roll-Up

| Specialist | Verdict Line | Biggest Finding |
|---|---|---|
| governance-data-triage | Sufficient data to run all six specialists, with a partial-data cap on ownership/insider-transaction detail; hard disqualifier already flagged upstream | Auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain (€440–770m contingent liability) locks the downstream verdict regardless of the operating record |
| management-and-track-record | Split-character record: stable founder-CEO who reliably hits guided Adjusted EBITDA, running a company whose statutory bottom line and cash flow remain fragile and one-off-dependent | Management Quality capped at 60/100 — the "path to profitability" is proven on Adjusted EBITDA for 3 years but has only 1 clean year on statutory operating profit (RF-MGT-004) |
| capital-allocation-scorecard | Value-destructive / size-driven | €2,054.0m cumulative goodwill impairment FY2021–2025 against the Woowa and Glovo mega-deals, alongside 49.6% share-count dilution with zero buybacks in any year — Capital Allocation scored 40/100 |
| incentives-and-compensation | Mixed — a genuinely improved FY2024 framework (70% LTI on relative TSR) layered on a multi-year legacy of pure growth-for-growth's-sake option grants still vesting through 2029 | The change-of-control provision hands full, unconditional equity vesting to Management Board members on resignation, independent of the per-share price achieved for other shareholders (RF-CAP-001) |
| ownership-and-insider-behavior | Thin, entirely compensation-derived insider ownership (0.38%); no pledging; the dominant current fact is a live control-structure event, not steady-state ownership | Uber holds 37% economic exposure to DHER and reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers has irrevocably committed to tender its remaining stake, which would push Uber's ownership "over 50%" |
| board-and-shareholder-rights | Adequate on paper (one-share-one-vote, no poison pill, immaterial RPT) but the real minority-holder risk is forward-looking | Uber's stated intent to "evaluate the most appropriate ownership structure" once its stake exceeds 50% raises live squeeze-out/domination-agreement risk for remaining minority holders |
| candor-and-disclosure-quality | Mixed — candid and specific under direct analyst challenge, but the unprompted framing consistently favors the flattering non-GAAP register | The absolute FY2025 statutory net loss (-€782.9m) was never spoken aloud on the earnings call — only the €183m year-over-year improvement was verbalized (RF-DISC-001) |

## 3. Reconciliation

Two disagreements are worth stating explicitly, both resolved conservatively:

1. **Capital allocation vs. incentive alignment.** `02_capital-allocation-scorecard` scores the historical record 40/100 (value-destructive), while `03_incentives-and-compensation` scores the FY2024-forward incentive framework 57/100 (mixed, improving). These are not contradictory once the timelines are separated: the capital-allocation score is a five-year backward look (FY2021–FY2025) dominated by the Woowa/Glovo mega-deal write-offs and the legacy revenue-CAGR option plan, while the incentive score credits a genuinely better system adopted starting FY2024 that has not yet had a full four-year cycle to prove itself. The reconciled view: the NEW framework is a real improvement but is one year old and untested, and it does not retroactively repair the value already destroyed under the OLD framework whose legacy options continue vesting through 2029.

2. **Board/shareholder-rights formal architecture vs. forward risk.** `05_board-and-shareholder-rights` finds the formal minority-protection architecture "genuinely clean" (one-share-one-vote, no poison pill, immaterial RPT) yet still scores the module's headline read as "adequate" rather than clean, because the pending Uber tender's squeeze-out/domination-agreement exposure is the dominant live risk. `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior` similarly declines to score the Uber process as a governance positive or negative on its own terms, treating it as a live event to monitor rather than a verdict input. The reconciled, more conservative view carried into this synthesis: today's formal architecture is real and better than a promoter-controlled or dual-class peer, but it provides no assurance about the terms on which any remaining minority stake would be compressed or cashed out if the deal closes without a full squeeze-out — that outcome is controlled by Uber and German takeover law, not by DHER's current board.

No other material disagreements exist between specialists; all six converge on describing FY2024 as an inflection point (better incentive design, better malus/clawback coverage, better financial-linkage in the STI) that is real but recent, sitting on top of a longer record (M&A, dilution, candor, board churn) that has not yet been offset by it.

**Forensic tag propagation (CLAUDE.md §13; eval check AQ).** `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md` fired both `RF-DISC-001` (management commentary contradicting/omitting the numbers — the absolute FY2025 statutory net loss was never spoken aloud, and the "profitable growth" claim was never qualified as Adjusted-EBITDA-specific vs. GAAP) and `RF-DISC-002` (recurring "one-off" non-GAAP add-backs — goodwill impairment in every one of the last 5 fiscal years, GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA gap 66%+ of the adjusted figure in both years reviewed) as standalone lines. Both are propagated here (Section 4 above and the Red-Flag Register, Section 5A) for the master synthesizer's cross-module forensic roll-up, alongside `earnings/06`'s earnings-quality findings and `balance-sheet-survival/05`'s findings, to detect a compounding accounting-integrity mosaic.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

This management team has delivered on the promise it controls most tightly — Adjusted EBITDA — hitting its own guided target within a percentage point or two in each of the last three fiscal years, a real and checkable record. It has not delivered per-share value: five years of capital deployment, dominated by two debt-and-stock-funded mega-acquisitions (Woowa, Glovo), left the company with €2,054m of cumulative goodwill write-offs, book value per share down more than three-quarters, tangible book value negative in every year, and nearly 50% more shares outstanding with no offsetting buyback. Incentives point in a better direction than they did — the FY2024 redesign ties 70% of the long-term plan to relative shareholder return and extended clawback to cover both annual and long-term pay for the first time — but that framework is one year old, sits behind a large legacy option pool that rewarded pure top-line growth through years of billion-euro losses, and insiders hold just 0.38% of the company, entirely from compensation rather than purchase. Minority shareholders' formal rights are real (single share class, no poison pill, immaterial related-party leakage) but the protection those rights deliver now depends on how a pending third-party acquisition plays out, not on anything the current board can guarantee, and management's own equity vests in full on a change of control — a conflict of interest layered directly on top of the live process. The single most important reason not to extend this team the benefit of the doubt with new capital is structural, not behavioral: an auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain, tied to a contingent liability of up to €770m, sits underneath a five-year capital-allocation record that already shows the company overpaid for the businesses that created that liability.

## 5D. Analyst Follow-Up Questions

- **On the going-concern note (Glovo Spain, RF-DISQ, business-model/01):** Is the €440–770m contingent liability recurring/structural (labor-classification exposure across the rider-economy business model) or a one-off legacy issue from a pre-acquisition business practice? Is it material to Group liquidity if it crystallizes at the high end? Does the FY2025 audited filing (not yet in the pool) show the same emphasis-of-matter language, an improvement, or an escalation?
- **On the serial-acquirer pattern (RF-CAP-004):** Was the Glovo/Woowa overpayment company-specific execution failure, or a sector-wide 2020–2021 growth-at-any-price valuation error that most food-delivery M&A of that vintage shares? Does the €2,054m impairment fully reflect the loss, or could further Woowa-specific write-downs still be pending (Finding 02-005, Insufficient Data)?
- **On the change-of-control acceleration (RF-CAP-001):** Did the Supervisory Board negotiate any price-linked conditionality into the Uber deal terms that offsets management's personal incentive to see the deal close regardless of price? Is this company-specific or standard-form German market practice for SE change-of-control provisions?
- **On the candor findings (RF-DISC-001, RF-DISC-002):** Is the pattern of leading with Adjusted EBITDA while omitting the absolute statutory net loss from prepared remarks a company-specific communication choice, or consistent with sector-wide non-GAAP practice among loss-making consumer-internet platforms? Does the recurring goodwill-impairment pattern reflect genuinely independent annual impairment testing, or a systematic pattern of initial overpayment being walked back year by year?
- **On the forward squeeze-out risk (05-021, RF-OWN-004 watch item):** What tender-acceptance threshold would trigger a full squeeze-out (95%) versus leave a partial minority stub under a ~75%-threshold domination agreement? Does German takeover law give remaining minority holders an appraisal-rights mechanism, and at what implied valuation?

## 6. What Would Change The Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Serious governance concerns | Glovo Spain contingent liability resolved/settled at the low end with no further escalation; FY2025 audited Annual Report confirms the going-concern language has been lifted; the Uber deal closes with a clean full squeeze-out (95%+) or an appraisal-rights mechanism that fairly prices remaining minority shares; FY2026 shows a second consecutive year of clean (statutory, not just Adjusted EBITDA) operating profit without one-off flattering items | The €440–770m Glovo Spain liability crystallizes at or near the high end; a second major goodwill impairment in FY2026 repeats the Woowa/Glovo pattern; the Uber deal closes below the squeeze-out threshold, leaving a minority stub under a value-maximizing parent with no clear protection | FY2025 audited Annual Report (not yet in the pool); the final Uber tender-acceptance rate and deal-structure outcome; a PDMR insider-transaction log for 2025–2026; confirmation of the FY2024 STI metric selection and the FY2024 Compensation Report say-on-pay vote outcome (AGM Jun 18, 2025) |

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict: Serious governance concerns.** The strongest single piece of evidence is the auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain (contingent liability €440–770m, Group opinion unmodified) — a hard disqualifier owned by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`, reported here verbatim and not re-adjudicated.
- **Capital-allocation record: value-destructive.** €2,054.0m of cumulative goodwill impairment (FY2021–2025) against the Woowa (~$4.0bn) and Glovo (€2.3bn) mega-deals; book value per share down 75.4%; tangible book value per share negative every year; 49.6% share-count growth with zero buybacks in any year. The one genuinely value-creative event in the period was the Talabat 20% IPO placement (~€1.8bn realized, control retained).
- **Incentive/ownership alignment: partial, improving, unproven.** The FY2024 redesign (70% of LTI on relative TSR, malus/clawback extended to both STI and LTI) is real but one year old; insiders hold only 0.38% of the company, entirely compensation-derived, not bought; the change-of-control provision gives management a personal, price-independent incentive to see the pending Uber deal close.
- **Biggest governance risk / severity:** The Glovo Spain going-concern note (Critical, hard disqualifier, verbatim below) compounded by the live Uber tender's forward squeeze-out/domination-agreement risk to remaining minority holders (High materiality, RF-OWN-004 watch item, not yet triggered).
- **Hard disqualifier flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan (verbatim):** "KPMG AG's Independent Auditor's Report carries a dedicated section titled 'Material Uncertainty about the Ability of Subsidiaries to Continue as a Going Concern,' tied to Glovoapp Spain Platform S.L.U. and the ongoing Spanish social-security rider-classification investigation (contingent liability disclosed at €440–770 million). [FY24 Annual Report, Independent Auditor's Report, p.222–223] The report explicitly states the group-level opinions 'have not been modified in this regard,' so this is a subsidiary-level material-uncertainty (going-concern) paragraph inside an otherwise unmodified/unqualified opinion — not a qualified opinion and not a going-concern doubt over the whole Group."
- **§24 rejector filters tripped:** Filter 2 (turnaround without ≥2–3 yrs proven inflection, RF-MGT-004) — Management Quality capped at 60. Filter 4 (serial-acquirer pattern, RF-CAP-004) — Capital Allocation capped at 50 (actual score 40, below the cap) and Governance Risk floored at 60 (superseded by the disqualifier's higher floor of 80). Filters 1 (integrity), 6 (unaligned owner) — not tripped as of report date; Filter 6 is a live forward watch item tied to the Uber deal's closing structure, not a current trigger.
- **Partial-data cap applied:** Shareholder friendliness capped at 60/100 (raw pre-cap score from `05` was 67) because the pool lacks a full beneficial-ownership table and a PDMR (insider) buy/sell transaction log — only the sole >10%-of-voting-rights WpHG notification (Naspers/MIH) is reproduced in the filing.
- **Biggest missing data point (single highest-value next request):** A PDMR (insider) transaction disclosure log covering 2025–2026 (Management Board and Supervisory Board buys/sells), to convert the currently unverified web-sourced narrative (an activist fund building to ~15% and reportedly pressuring the founder-CEO to step down shortly before the Uber bid emerged) into primary evidence — this bears directly on whether insiders were selling ahead of, or buying into, the events that led to the takeover offer.
- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance` quick-read. The master synthesizer should treat THIS module's governance verdict (Serious governance concerns) and scores (Governance Score 50/100, Confidence-Adjusted 36/100, Rating Weak) as the primary governance read.

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management has kept its own profit promise (Adjusted EBITDA) three years running, but that promise excludes the losses and write-offs that matter most to a shareholder.
- Capital has destroyed per-share value: the company wrote off €2.05 billion on acquisitions it overpaid for, book value per share fell three-quarters, and the number of shares outstanding grew by half with no share buybacks to offset it.
- Pay now rewards relative stock performance (70% of long-term pay) for the first time, but a big pool of older stock options still vesting through 2029 rewarded pure revenue growth through years of billion-euro losses.
- Insiders own very little of the company — 0.38%, and every share of it came from pay packages, not money the founder or other executives put in themselves; there is no share pledging.
- Minority shareholders' formal rights look clean on paper (one share, one vote, no poison pill), but their real protection now hinges on how a pending takeover by Uber plays out, not on anything the current board controls.
- Management is honest when directly pressed by analysts, but consistently leads with the flattering non-GAAP number and never once said the size of the year's actual loss out loud on the earnings call.
- A hard disqualifier is flagged: the auditors themselves raised a going-concern warning on a Spanish subsidiary tied to a legal dispute worth up to €770 million.
- This module is highly useful for the master synthesizer — it supplies the primary governance verdict, the evidence for the disqualifier's downstream lock, and the specific change-of-control conflict of interest relevant to the live Uber deal.

```red_flags.csv
red_flag_id,trigger,severity,evidence,source_date,score_impact,follow_up
RF-MGT-004,"Turnaround claimed without 2-3 yrs delivered operating inflection on statutory metric (SS24 Filter 2)",High,"Only 1 fiscal year (FY2025) of positive GAAP operating profit after 4 years of losses (FY2021-2024); Adjusted EBITDA hit 3 years running but statutory profit has only 1 clean year, flattered by one-offs in adjacent periods","FY24 Annual Report; business-model/07_business-quality.md, 2026-08-12",-4 (Management Quality capped at 60/100),"Confirm FY2026 statutory operating profit sustains without one-off flattering items"
RF-CAP-004,"Serial-acquirer / value-destructive M&A pattern (CLAUDE.md Section24 Filter 4)",High,"EUR857.8m Glovo-linked goodwill impairment in FY2023 alone; EUR2,054.0m cumulative group goodwill impairment FY2021-2025 against Woowa (~USD4.0bn) and Glovo (EUR2.3bn) mega-deals","FY24 Annual Report, Note F.1.b, p.161, 2025-04-25",-14 (Capital Allocation capped at max 50; actual score 40 sits below the cap),"Monitor FY2026 for a repeat pattern; confirm Woowa-specific impairment if disclosed"
RF-CAP-001,"Large corporate-action incentive not tied to shareholder price outcome",High,"Management Board members may resign within 3 months of a change of control with all outstanding STI/LTI incentive instruments fully vesting immediately, unconditional on the per-share price achieved for other shareholders -- live and material given the pending Uber tender","FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.39-40, 2025-04-25",-6 (Incentive Alignment, long-term-orientation component),"Confirm whether the Supervisory Board negotiated price-linked conditionality into the Uber transaction terms"
RF-DISC-001,"Management commentary contradicts / omits the numbers",High,"CFO's entire verbal disclosure of the FY2025 net-income line was 'the net result improved by EUR183 million' -- the absolute -EUR782.9m loss was never spoken aloud; CEO's 'we did both... profitable growth' claim rests entirely on Adjusted EBITDA (+30%) with GAAP operating profit (EUR93.7m) unmentioned in prepared remarks","FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, p.4-5",-5 each (Disclosure Candor, owning-the-misses component),"Check FY2026 call for whether the absolute net result and GAAP operating profit are stated unprompted"
RF-DISC-002,"Recurring 'one-off' / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs",High,"Goodwill impairment recorded in every one of the last 5 fiscal years (2021-2025), never zero; GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA gap is 66% of the adjusted figure in FY2025 and exceeds 100% in FY2024 -- both far above the 15%-of-GAAP-earnings scrutiny threshold","earnings/06_earnings-quality.md Section4-5, Section7-8, 2026-08-12",-6 each (Disclosure Candor, non-GAAP-discipline component),"Monitor FY2026 for a break in the recurring-impairment pattern"
```

# Governance Data Triage — DHER

**Context note (carried from `business-model/00_data-triage` and `01_disqualifier-scan`):** Delivery Hero SE is currently the subject of a live, announced acquisition offer from Uber Technologies (M&A call held Jul 16, 2026), with reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers "irrevocably committed to tender its stake" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE - M&A Call, Jul 16 2026, p.4]. This is not a steady-state standalone company at report date — later specialists should read the stewardship record with that overlay. Separately, `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` triggered a hard disqualifier (auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain tied to a €440–770m contingent social-security liability; Group opinion unmodified) — see Section 6.

For a German SE under the two-tier board system, the local proxy-equivalent is the Annual Report's Corporate Governance Statement + Compensation Report + Report of the Supervisory Board (all present and bundled in one document); the ownership-equivalent is the WpHG voting-rights notification disclosure (present, but only above the 10% threshold as reproduced in-document); the board-equivalent is the Supervisory Board (Aufsichtsrat) composition and independence assessment (present in detail). No US DEF 14A / Form 4 / 10-K should be expected or marked "missing" — none of the local equivalents are absent.

All documents in this pool are in English; no translation issue arises. Noted per instructions — no downgrade applied and none would apply even for a non-English filing.

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict:** Sufficient
- **Reason:** A proxy-equivalent/compensation disclosure (Annual Report's Compensation Report + Corporate Governance Statement), an ownership disclosure (WpHG >10% voting-rights notification plus a live M&A-call tender-commitment signal), and a board/related-party disclosure (Supervisory Board composition + independence + Note 10) are all present, together with a six-year (2020–2025) capital-allocation history in the Capital IQ workbooks — so all six specialists can run.
- **Specialists that can run:** management track record (01), capital allocation (02), incentives/compensation (03), ownership and insider behavior (04 — with the cap below), board and shareholder rights (05), candor and disclosure quality (06)
- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan?** Y — auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note (Glovo Spain, social-security investigation, contingent liability €440–770m; Group-level opinion unmodified) [FY24 Annual Report, Independent Auditor's Report, pp.222–223]. Per MODULE_RULES.md Disqualifier Deference and Score Cap Rules, downstream synthesis must apply the Governance-risk floor (≥80) and cap the stewardship verdict at no better than "Serious governance concerns," regardless of the otherwise-detailed disclosure record documented above.
- **Active partial-data caps:**
  - Ownership/insider-transaction read is limited to the single >10% WpHG-notification holder (Naspers/MIH Food Holdings) as reproduced in the Annual Report; no full beneficial-ownership table and no PDMR buy/sell transaction log are in the pool — apply **Shareholder friendliness max 60** per MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules ("No ownership / insider-transaction data").
- **Critical missing items:**
  - No PDMR (insider) transaction log — only the MAR Article 19(11) closed-period obligation is disclosed, not actual buy/sell records.
  - No full AGM voting-results table across all resolutions (only the Compensation Report 2023 approval vote, 93.05%, is quoted).
  - No FY2025 audited Annual Report — compensation, board, and related-party disclosures rest on FY2024 data (~19.4 months stale at period-end), one full fiscal year behind the transcript-reported FY2025 actuals.
- **Single highest-value missing document:** A PDMR (insider) transaction disclosure log covering 2025–2026 (buys/sells by Management Board and Supervisory Board members), to give the ownership specialist (04) a direct conviction signal ahead of the Uber tender offer.

## management-governance / 01_management-and-track-record.md

_Source: `01_management-and-track-record.md`_

# Management & Track Record — DHER

**Context carried from `management-governance/00_governance-data-triage.md` and `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`:** Delivery Hero is currently the subject of a live, announced Uber Technologies acquisition offer (M&A call, Jul 16, 2026; expected close H2 2027), with reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers "irrevocably committed to tender its stake" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE - M&A Call, Jul 16 2026, p.4]. This is not a steady-state read of a standalone company — the record below is what management actually delivered, but the current moment is a corporate-action overlay, not business-as-usual. Separately, `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` triggered a hard disqualifier (auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain tied to a €440–770m contingent social-security liability; Group opinion unmodified) — this agent does not re-adjudicate that lock (Disqualifier Deference) but the fact bears on how conservatively "management competence" should be read here.

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CEO / Chair of Management Board | Niklas Östberg | Co-founder and CEO since the company's founding in 2011; first appointed to the Management Board in 2018 upon conversion to a Societas Europaea (SE) | ~15 years as CEO (2011–present); ~8 years as a formally appointed Management Board member | Founder-operator; has run the company continuously through its transition from a European food-delivery start-up to a ~70-country, ~€48.8bn-GMV marketplace-and-quick-commerce group, including the FY2024 talabat IPO and the current Uber acquisition process | FY24 Annual Report, Management Board section, p.10 & p.327; FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, speaker credit "Co-Founder, MD, CEO, Chairman of the Management Board" |
| CFO | Marie-Anne Popp | Named interim CFO effective Jul 1, 2024; appointed permanent CFO and Management Board member Jan 2025 | ~1.6 years in the CFO seat (interim from Jul 2024, permanent from Jan 2025) as of 2026-08-12 | Filing does not detail her pre-Delivery Hero career; web-sourced (unverified): prior senior roles at Adidas and General Electric, degrees from Harvard Business School and ESCP Business School | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(a) Related-Party Disclosures, p.207; Web: RTTNews / MarketScreener coverage of the Jan-2025 CFO appointment, retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified, background claim only) |
| COO / Management Board member | Pieter-Jan Vandepitte | COO since Aug 1, 2015; first appointed to the Management Board in 2021 for an initial five-year term (through Apr 30, 2026) | ~11 years as COO; ~5 years on the Management Board, contract renewed a further 3 years to Apr 30, 2029 | Long-tenured operator who ran business divisions through the company's international expansion before joining the Board | FY24 Annual Report, Corporate Governance Statement, p.944–948; Web: Delivery Hero newsroom coverage of the Jan-2026 service-agreement renewal, retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified) |
| CPO / Management Board member | Johannes Bruder | Joined the Management Board Jan 1, 2026 (was already Chief Product Officer) | ~0.6 years on the Board as of 2026-08-12; longer tenure as CPO not stated in the pool | Responsible for the company's unified global technology platform and AI-driven automation rollout, per the company's own account | Web: Delivery Hero newsroom, "Delivery Hero appoints new Management Board member," retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified) — not in the FY24 Annual Report, which predates this appointment |

**Founder / owner-operator status.** Niklas Östberg is the company's co-founder and has been CEO continuously since founding in 2011 — this is a founder-CEO, not a hired external operator. He does not, however, control the company through a dual-class or super-voting structure: the FY24 Annual Report states "there are no different share classes... no shares with special rights conferring powers of control" [FY24 Annual Report, p.32]. His alignment therefore rests on tenure and (per the separate ownership specialist, `04`) whatever personal shareholding he holds, not on a control block.

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | CFO Emmanuel Thomassin — CFO since 2018, "who had been Chief Financial Officer since 2018" and "accompanied the Company for more than ten years" in other roles — resigned "in agreement with the Supervisory Board" effective Jun 30, 2024. This left a two-person Management Board (CEO + COO only) for six months until Marie-Anne Popp was named interim CFO Jul 1, 2024, and a formal Management Board seat was not filled until Jan 2025 — roughly a **seven-month gap** in a full three-person board. | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(a)–(b), p.206–207; FY24 Annual Report, "Personnel matters of the Management Board," p.656–676 |
| CFO turnover | One CFO change in the period covered by the pool (Thomassin → Popp, 2024–2025). No pattern of repeated CFO turnover is evidenced — this is a single transition, handled with an internal interim step rather than an external emergency hire, and the Supervisory Board publicly thanked the departing CFO for his contribution rather than describing a forced exit. Still a real stability gap given the multi-month vacancy at Management Board level. | FY24 Annual Report, p.670–676 |
| Supervisory Board churn | The 8-member Supervisory Board saw a new Chair (Kristin Skogen Lund, from Jun 19, 2024) plus at least four other seat changes within calendar-2024 alone: Deputy Chair Patrick Kolek left Apr 30, 2024; Jeanette L. Gorgas left Jun 19, 2024; Scott Ferguson (Sachem Head Capital, an activist fund) joined Jun 26, 2024; Roger Rabalais (Prosus Group operating partner) served only May 1–Dec 31, 2024 before departing. That is roughly half the Supervisory Board turning over inside one year. | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(b), p.206–207 |
| CEO succession / continuity | No CEO change. Östberg has led the company since founding (2011) and remains Chairman of the Management Board as of the most recent transcript (Mar 26, 2026). His and COO Vandepitte's service agreements were both renewed by the Supervisory Board for a further three years (to Apr 30, 2029) in a decision reported around Jan 2026 — a positive continuity signal, though sourced to web coverage rather than a pool filing (the pool's FY24 Annual Report predates this event). | FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, speaker credits; Web: Delivery Hero newsroom, Jan-2026 service-agreement renewal, retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified) |

**Warning flag.** A seven-month partial Management Board vacancy at the CFO level, layered with roughly half the Supervisory Board turning over in the same twelve months (2024), is a material stability signal — even though the CFO transition itself was handled without apparent scandal (an internal interim promotion, not an emergency external search) and the founder-CEO provided continuity throughout. This is monitored, not treated as disqualifying on its own.

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management describes the strategy as defending and growing local-delivery category leadership while "becoming an everyday app": expanding quick commerce (Dmarts) beyond food into grocery, health and beauty; scaling AdTech toward a stated "€1.5 billion revenue run rate"; migrating remaining brands onto a single global technology stack; and applying AI to cut cost-to-serve (a stated target of automating 85% of first-line customer-service contacts) [FY2025 Earnings Call, Mar 26 2026, prepared remarks]. The explicit, checkable FY2026 targets management has set are: Group GMV growth of 8%–10% like-for-like; Adjusted EBITDA of €910m–€960m; and Free Cash Flow of more than €200m, the last two stated on a basis that excludes "extraordinary" items such as legal costs, the Talabat minority dividend, and related interest [FY2025 Earnings Call, Mar 26 2026, prepared remarks; Q&A, CFO Marie-Anne Popp]. No formal revenue or EPS figure is guided.

## 4. Promise vs Delivery

| Prior Promise / Target | Period Set | Actual Outcome | Kept / Missed | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Adjusted EBITDA €250m | Guided at/around FY2022 reporting, for FY2023 | €253.6m actual | **Kept** (+1.4%) | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6, citing DeliveryHeroSEXTRADHEREstimatesReport.xls, Guidance tab |
| Adjusted EBITDA €693m | Guided for FY2024 | €692.5m actual | **Kept** (in-line, -0.1%) | Same source |
| Adjusted EBITDA €900m–€940m | Guided for FY2025 | €903m actual (landed near the low end of the range) | **Kept**, near the floor | Same source |
| Free Cash Flow €50m–€100m | Guided for FY2024 | €358.8m reported | **"Beat" headline-wise**, but inflated by a €220.9m non-operating gain (Uber's Foodpanda-Taiwan-deal breakup fee) plus a €99.3m debt-modification gain — not a clean like-for-like beat on the operating business | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6; `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`, one-off gains row |
| Free Cash Flow ~€120m (point guide) | Guided for FY2025 | -€246m reported | **Missed** by ~€366m on the headline (reported) figure — though management states the guide itself excludes "extraordinary outflows" the reported figure includes, so the miss is partly definitional, not purely operational | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §2, §6, citing CFO Marie-Anne Popp Q&A |
| "2025 goal in Korea is clearly set out: to regain growth" | CEO letter, FY2024 Annual Report (published Apr 2025) | FY2025 Earnings Call: "return momentum in Korea and Saudi Arabia towards the end of the year"; Q1 2026 call confirms the improvement "continued into Q1" | **Kept**, but achieved late in the promised year, not throughout it | FY24 Annual Report, CEO letter, p.9; FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, p.1 & p.8–9 |
| GMV growth 8%–10% like-for-like, FY2026 | Guided Mar 26 2026 | Q1 2026 actual: 8.8% like-for-like (up from 7.9% in Q4 2025) — inside the range, toward the low end | **In progress / on track**, too early to score as kept or missed for the full year | Q1 2026 Sales/Trading Statement Call, Apr 30 2026 |
| Street EPS Normalized estimate (not a company promise — a consensus benchmark) | FY2021–FY2025 | Missed Street's estimate in every one of the last five reported fiscal years, including FY2025 where the Street expected a near-breakeven result and got a loss | **Missed, 5 years straight** — a candor/execution-context signal even though the company itself does not guide EPS | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6 |

**Read on the record.** On the metric management itself sets and controls closely — Adjusted EBITDA — the record over three straight fiscal years (FY2023–FY2025) is strong: guided, then delivered, within a percentage point or two each time. On Free Cash Flow, the record is materially worse: FY2024's headline "beat" and FY2025's headline "miss" both trace largely to non-operating, one-off items (an Uber breakup fee, a debt-modification gain, litigation/dividend outflows) rather than a clean read of underlying cash generation — management explains the gap when asked, but the guidance basis ("before extraordinary items") does not match the reported "Actual" figure the market sees, which is a genuine comparability problem investors have to correct for themselves. The five-year Street EPS miss streak is a further caution against taking the calm, low-activity current FY2026 estimate revisions (`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §4–5) as reassurance.

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | Partially. There is no new-management story — Östberg has been founder-CEO throughout — but the company is executing a self-declared, multi-year "path to profitability" plan after years of statutory losses, and `business-model/07_business-quality.md` explicitly frames FY2025's first-ever Group operating profit (€93.7m, 0.7% margin) as "a fragile, recent inflection... not a cyclical peak to anchor against." This is closer to an operator-driven turnaround than an outsider-turnaround, but the same evidentiary bar applies: has the improvement actually been delivered, not just planned? | `business-model/07_business-quality.md` §(Filter 5 discussion, industry rate-of-change) |
| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | Mixed, by metric. On the company's own preferred non-GAAP metric (Adjusted EBITDA), there are **3 consecutive fiscal years (FY2023–FY2025) of guided targets actually hit** — a real, evidenced multi-year record on that line. On statutory/GAAP operating profit, there is only **1 year** (FY2025) of positive results after a long loss history, and that year's underlying trend is muddied by one-off gains in the prior year (FY2024) and a goodwill impairment in the current year (FY2025) — see `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`. This falls short of the §24 Filter 2 bar of "2–3 years of proven operating improvement" on the metric that actually matters (statutory profitability, not the company-defined adjusted metric). | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6; `business-model/07_business-quality.md`; `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | No — this is not a case of a newly hired "star CEO" résumé being offered as the reason to believe (Östberg is the same founder who has run the company since 2011, not an external turnaround hire). The evidence offered is the guidance track record itself (Section 4), which is a stronger basis than a résumé, but it is still concentrated on a non-GAAP metric with only one clean year on the statutory bottom line. | Inference, not from filings — drawn from the absence of any "new leadership" or "new plan" framing in the pool materials |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (unverified adverse signal) | None found. The disqualifier scan's only trigger is the auditor going-concern note on Glovo Spain (a subsidiary-level legal/contingent-liability matter, not a personal-integrity allegation against management), and `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` raises disclosed cybersecurity incidents and one-off accounting items — none of which is framed as unverified "buzz" about a named executive. No RF-MGT-005 applies. | `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`; `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` |

**Turnaround rule applied (Filter 2).** Because the proof of delivered operating inflection is real but metric-dependent (3 years on Adjusted EBITDA, only 1 year on statutory operating profit, and that year flattered by one-offs in adjacent periods), this report treats "path to profitability" as **not yet proven on a GAAP basis** and applies the MODULE_RULES base-rate penalty: **Management Quality is capped at 60/100**, and any downstream thesis that leans on "Delivery Hero has turned the corner" should be classified honestly as resting partly on a governance-turnaround claim that is still short of the §24 Filter 2 bar on the metric that matters most.

**Integrity rule applied (Filter 1).** No unresolved adverse integrity signal was routed to this agent. `RF-MGT-005` is **not emitted**.

## 5. Management Read

Niklas Östberg has run this company for fifteen years as founder-CEO with no leadership discontinuity, and his and COO Vandepitte's contracts were both extended to 2029 in early 2026 — a real continuity signal, not narrative. Set against that: a seven-month partial Management Board vacancy at CFO after Emmanuel Thomassin's 2024 departure, and roughly half the Supervisory Board turning over in the same year, are stability warnings that should not be waved away by the founder's tenure. On delivery, management has hit its own guided Adjusted EBITDA target three years running — real, checkable execution on the metric it controls — but the Free Cash Flow guidance has swung from a large headline beat to a large headline miss largely on non-operating items, and the Street has missed on EPS every year for five straight years; the "path to profitability" story is therefore proven on one company-chosen metric and still unproven on the metric (statutory operating profit/cash flow) that ultimately matters to a shareholder. The single biggest management signal is the split character of the record: a stable, tenured founder who reliably hits his own guided operating target, running a company whose reported bottom-line and cash-flow numbers remain fragile, one-off-dependent, and one year — not several — into an inflection that has not yet been proven durable.

| Transparency around misses | 6 | 10 | CFO explicitly explained the FCF guidance-vs-reported basis gap when questioned on the FY2025 earnings call — a real attempt at candor — but the practical effect (a guide basis that excludes items the reported "Actual" figure includes) creates recurring headline-miss risk that a plainer guidance basis would avoid | 
| **Total** | **60** | **100** | **Capped at 60/100 per the §24 Filter 2 turnaround rule (RF-MGT-004): the "path to profitability" narrative is proven on the company's own Adjusted EBITDA metric for 3 years but not yet proven on statutory operating profit/cash flow, which has only 1 clean year and is flattered by one-off items in adjacent periods.** |

**Partial-data cap applied:** none beyond the turnaround-rule cap above — prior shareholder letters (the CEO letter substitutes under German AktG practice), transcripts, and guidance data are all present in this pool, so the promise-vs-delivery record above is assessed directly, not capped for data absence.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — DHER

**Context carried from `01_management-and-track-record.md` and `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`:** Delivery Hero SE (Frankfurt Prime Standard, XTRA:DHER; German Societas Europaea, IFRS as adopted by the EU, reporting currency EUR, fiscal year end 31-Dec) is, at this report's date, the subject of a live, announced acquisition offer from Uber Technologies (M&A call, 16-Jul-2026), with reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers "irrevocably committed to tender its stake" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE – M&A Call, 16-Jul-2026, transcript p.4]. This is a look-back scorecard on capital already spent by a still-independent, standalone board — it is not affected by the pending deal, but the pending deal is itself a data point about where five years of that capital allocation left the company (Section 5). The most recent audited source in the pool is the FY2024 Annual Report (published 25-Apr-2025); FY2025 figures come from the 26-Mar-2026 earnings-call transcript (management-stated) and the Capital IQ workbook export, cross-checked, not from an audited filing — flagged wherever it matters. This module deepens and supersedes `business-model/11`'s quick-read per `MODULE_RULES.md`.

## 1. Uses of Capital (5 years, FY2021–FY2025)

Currency: **EUR million**, cumulative FY2021–FY2025 (5 fiscal years), reporting standard IFRS as adopted by the EU [Delivery Hero SE XTRA DHER Financials.xls, Cash Flow tab; `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` §1].

**Denominator note (§15 hygiene):** cumulative CFO over this window is **−€892.9m** and cumulative FCF is **−€2,329.6m** — both negative, because the company was cash-flow-negative in three of the five years (FY2021–FY2023) before turning CFO-positive in FY2024–FY2025. Dividing a cash outflow by a negative CFO/FCF base produces a ratio with the wrong sign and no economic meaning (e.g. capex ÷ CFO = 1,437.7 ÷ −892.9 = −161%, which reads as "capex more than fully funded by operations" when the opposite is true). The table below states the ratio to CFO/FCF where it is directly interpretable and marks it "Not meaningful (negative base)" where it is not; a cumulative-revenue column is added alongside as the more honest scale reference.

| Use | Cumulative Amount (FY21–25) | Currency | % of Cumulative CFO/FCF | % of Cumulative Revenue (€50,729.1m) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|
| Acquisitions, gross cash paid | −1,559.9 | EUR m | Not meaningful (negative CFO base) | 3.1% | Cash Flow tab, "Cash Acquisitions" [1] |
| Divestiture proceeds | +658.9 | EUR m | Not meaningful | 1.3% | Cash Flow tab, "Divestitures" [1] |
| **Net M&A cash flow** | **−901.0** | EUR m | Not meaningful | 1.8% | Computed: Cash Acquisitions + Divestitures [1] |
| Buybacks (own common equity) | 0.0 | EUR m | 0.0% (no buybacks in any of 5 years) | 0.0% | Cash Flow tab — no "Repurchase of Common Stock" line item exists in the reported series for FY2020–FY2025; confirmed zero every year [1] |
| Dividends | 0.0 | EUR m | 0.0% | 0.0% | Cash Flow tab, "Total Dividends Paid" — blank/zero every year FY2020–FY2025 [1] |
| Capex (PP&E + capitalized intangibles) | −1,437.7 | EUR m | Not meaningful (negative CFO base); = 61.7% of cumulative FCF outflow | 2.8% | `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` §1 (normalized operating capex definition) |
| Debt repaid, gross | −3,405.8 | EUR m | Not meaningful | 6.7% | Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Repaid" [1] |
| Debt issued, gross | +3,313.0 | EUR m | Not meaningful | 6.5% | Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Issued" [1] |
| **Net debt repayment** | **−92.8** | EUR m | Not meaningful | 0.2% | Computed: Debt Issued − Debt Repaid [1] |
| Common-stock issuance (cash) | +3,395.3 | EUR m | Not meaningful | 6.7% | Cash Flow tab, "Issuance of Common Stock" — driven mainly by a €2,138.8m FY2024 figure this agent cannot cleanly attribute (possibly linked to the talabat listing's €1,803.5m equity reclassification plus ESPP/option exercises, but the pool does not reconcile the CIQ line to that specific event) — **flagged as an unresolved reconciliation gap**, not silently assumed [1][2] |

**Read:** over five years the company was a net cash *user*, not a net cash generator — cumulative operating cash flow was negative (−€892.9m) and the shortfall, plus M&A (−€901.0m net) and capex (−€1,437.7m), was funded by new debt (+€3,313.0m) and equity issuance (+€3,395.3m, largely unreconciled — see flag above) and by drawing down the cash balance built up from the 2020–2021 capital raises. There were **zero dollars spent on buybacks and zero dollars spent on dividends** in every one of the five years — the only two "clean" (no-negative-consequence) lines in this table are exactly the two the company chose not to use.

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| Woowa Brothers Corp. (South Korea, "Baemin") | Announced Dec-2019, closed 2020–2021 | **~$4.0bn total** ($1.9bn cash / $2.1bn stock, ≈€1.7bn cash / €1.9bn stock at announcement-date FX) [web-sourced, unverified — PYMNTS, Restaurant Dive, Verdict Food Service, retrieved 2026-08-12]; remaining 0.2% NCI bought for €11.0m in 2023 [FY24 Annual Report, Note "Disclosures on Participations", p.161] | Stated goal: expand into Asia and become the largest food-delivery provider outside China by order share [web-sourced, unverified] | **Not separately trackable.** No Woowa/Korea-specific goodwill-impairment line is disclosed in the FY24 Annual Report's CGU breakdown for FY2023–FY2025 (impairments there hit "Asia Delivery" CGU (€89.7m, FY2024) and Glovo/LatAm/Europe CGUs (FY2023), not a named "Woowa" CGU) — **Insufficient Data** to say definitively whether Woowa itself has been written down since acquisition. A Bloomberg Law report from 2022 flagged a potential ~$1.7bn impairment risk on the Woowa deal specifically [web-sourced, unverified, dated 2022, retrieved 2026-08-12] — this pool cannot confirm whether that risk crystallized as a Woowa-specific charge. Regulatory drag is confirmed: Korea's antitrust regulator forced Delivery Hero to divest its *own* pre-existing Korean unit before the deal could close [web-sourced, unverified] | To close the deal at all, DHER had to sell off its own existing Korean food-delivery business as an antitrust condition — a real, disclosed (web-sourced) forced divestiture directly caused by pursuing this acquisition | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Disclosures on Participations", p.161; Web (unverified, retrieved 2026-08-12): PYMNTS, Restaurant Dive, Verdict Food Service, Bloomberg Law |
| Glovoapp23 S.A. (Spain) — stake built from 43.8% (2018) to 99.4% (2024) | Majority-stake agreement 31-Dec-2021 (closed 2022); further NCI purchases through 2024 | Majority-stake tranche valued Glovo at **€2.3bn**; the incremental 39.4% was paid for with 7.9m new DHER shares (≈€774m in stock) plus up to €250m of backstop financing commitments [web-sourced, unverified — Bloomberg, TechCrunch, EU-Startups, retrieved 2026-08-12]. Ownership then rose to 99.2% (2023) and 99.4% (2024) via further purchases (amounts not separately itemized for these tranches in the FY24 Annual Report) | Stated goal: build the largest last-mile-delivery marketplace in Europe/Latin America under one platform [FY24 Annual Report, Group strategy narrative] | **No — evidenced by writedowns.** Glovo-linked cash-generating units (Glovo platform, Glovo Dmart, LatAm platform, Europe platform, Europe Dmart) absorbed **€857.8m of goodwill impairment in FY2023 alone** [FY24 Annual Report, Note F.1.b, p.~161], against a total group goodwill-impairment addition of €894.6m booked in the FY2023 intangibles-movement table [FY24 Annual Report, Intangible Assets note, p.166 — this agent's own read of the goodwill "Impairment losses" column for the "previous year" (FY2023) comparative]. The ~€37m gap between €857.8m (P&L-disclosed, CGU-attributed figure) and €894.6m (balance-sheet movement-table figure) is **flagged, not silently reconciled** — both come from the same audited filing and the difference is plausibly scope (e.g. FX-translation interaction with impairment) rather than error, but this agent cannot confirm which | Glovo Spain also carries a subsidiary-level auditor going-concern emphasis-of-matter tied to up to €770m of unrecognized rider-classification contingent liability (Section 4, off-balance-sheet) — a direct legal/financial-stability overhang traceable to the business this acquisition brought into the group, not a coincidence of timing | FY24 Annual Report, Note F.1.b, p.~161; Intangible Assets note, p.166; Web (unverified, retrieved 2026-08-12): Bloomberg, TechCrunch, EU-Startups, CB Insights |
| Hungerstation Holding Limited (UAE) — remaining 37.0% NCI | Closed 16-Jun-2023 | €276.8m | Consolidate full ownership of a MENA asset ahead of the later Talabat holding-company structure | **Arguably yes, in hindsight** — Hungerstation sits inside the talabat group, which was subsequently listed on the Dubai Financial Market at a valuation implying total placement proceeds of ~€1.8bn for just a 20% stake (Section 2, Talabat row below), suggesting the earlier 2023 buy-in was made ahead of a value-accretive event, not a value-destructive one | None identified — a clean, disclosed minority buy-in with no forced-sale evidence | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Acquisitions and Divestitures", p.161 |
| Woowa Brothers Corp. — remaining 0.2% NCI | Closed 27-Sep-2023 | €11.0m | Immaterial residual buy-in to reach full ownership | Immaterial — too small to assess return separately | None | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Acquisitions and Divestitures", p.161 |
| Worldcoo S.L. (Spain) | Closed 14-Mar-2023 | €10.6m | Not detailed beyond "acquired 100% of shares" | Immaterial — too small to assess return separately | None identified | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Acquisitions and Divestitures", p.161 |
| Foodpanda Taiwan + DH Stores Taiwan — sale **to** Uber (divestiture attempt) | Agreed 14-May-2024; blocked by Taiwan Fair Trade Commission; Share Purchase Agreement terminated 10-Mar-2025 | $950m agreed consideration (deal never closed) | Planned exit from a non-core Asian market to simplify the portfolio and realize cash | **Deal failed** — Taiwan's antitrust regulator refused approval and DHER kept the business; Uber paid a **€220.9m breakup fee** instead of the $950m sale proceeds, recognized as FY2024 other operating income — a real cash inflow, but a fraction of the intended proceeds and not a completed strategic exit | The attempted sale itself is evidence DHER was actively trying to shed non-core Asian assets by 2024 — a real, disclosed instance of portfolio pruning under pressure, that then failed for regulatory reasons outside management's control | FY24 Annual Report, p.161; `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` §one-off gains row |
| Talabat Holding plc (UAE/MENA) — 20% IPO placement (partial divestiture, retained control) | Listed Dubai Financial Market, 10-Dec-2024 | AED 1.60/share (€0.41/share); total placement volume AED 7.5bn (**≈€1.8bn**); net consideration recognized in equity €1,803.5m; DHER retained an **80% stake** | Realize value from a MENA subsidiary while keeping control; "enhanced our financial flexibility" [FY24 Annual Report, CEO letter, p.283] | **Yes — the cleanest value-realization event in the period.** DHER converted a fraction of an internally-built subsidiary into ~€1.8bn of cash/equity value at a market-clearing IPO price without losing control, a genuine capital-recycling success distinct from the mega-deal pattern above | None identified as a forced sale — management frames this as proactive, and the timing (ahead of the 2026 Uber approach) suggests balance-sheet strengthening rather than distress selling | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Disclosures on Participations", p.161; CEO letter, p.283 |
| Digital Services SG Four Pte. Ltd. (Singapore) | 2024 | Amount not disclosed in the pool | Loss of significant influence; entity deconsolidated as an associate | Not assessable — no price disclosed | Not assessable | FY24 Annual Report, Note "Acquisitions and Divestitures", p.161 |

**Serial-acquirer + opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4) — applied.** DHER built its current group through two debt-and-stock-funded mega-deals (Woowa ~$4.0bn, Glovo €2.3bn headline valuation, together the majority of the group's €4.4–5.0bn goodwill balance across FY2021–FY2025) plus a string of smaller bolt-ons and NCI buy-ins. Against those same deals, the group has recognized **€2,054.0m of cumulative goodwill impairment from FY2021–FY2025** (€85.9m + €760.9m + €857.8m/€894.6m + €89.7m + €259.7m, see the goodwill-impairment red-flag table already established in `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`) — direct, audited evidence that the group paid more for these businesses than they turned out to be worth. The opportunity cost is not confined to the write-downs themselves: to close the Woowa deal, DHER had to divest its own pre-existing Korean business as an antitrust condition (a forced divestiture directly caused by the acquisition, web-sourced); Glovo's rider-classification legal exposure (up to €770m unrecognized, Section 4) came bundled with the acquisition and now carries a subsidiary-level going-concern emphasis-of-matter; and the balance-sheet and per-share damage from the acquisition-and-dilution program (Section 3, Section 4) left the company financially stretched enough that, as of this report's date, its reference shareholder has agreed to sell the entire company to Uber rather than continue standalone — the ultimate foregone option is continued independence itself. **RF-CAP-004 is triggered.** Per `MODULE_RULES.md` Score Cap Rules, this caps the M&A-discipline component near the floor regardless of the two genuinely value-accretive moves in the period (the Hungerstation buy-in ahead of the Talabat listing, and the Talabat IPO itself) — those offset the M&A discipline score modestly upward from the floor but do not come close to lifting it out of the weak band. Bare "expand into Asia" / "strengthen the platform" language in the disclosed rationales is treated as narrative, not evidence, consistent with the rule that synergy language alone does not prove value creation.

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

**DHER has never repurchased its own common shares for cancellation in any year FY2020–FY2025.** The Cash Flow statement (Capital IQ standard template) carries no "Repurchase of Common Stock" line for this company because the value is zero in every year — confirmed against the raw workbook export [Delivery Hero SE XTRA DHER Financials.xls, Cash Flow tab]. There is therefore no equity-buyback price discipline to score in the conventional sense; the table below records that fact rather than fabricating a judgment on purchases that did not happen.

| Year | Amount Spent (equity buyback) | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change (period) | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2021 | 0.0 | EUR | 0 | N/A | 199.3m → 243.2m (+22.0%) | No — SBC expense €303.1m same year, zero offset | Not applicable — no purchases | No buyback to judge | [1] |
| FY2022 | 0.0 | EUR | 0 | N/A | 243.2m → 257.3m (+5.8%) | No — SBC expense €325.9m, zero offset | Not applicable | No buyback to judge | [1] |
| FY2023 | 0.0 | EUR | 0 | N/A | 257.3m → 269.9m (+4.9%) | No — SBC expense €247.4m, zero offset | Not applicable | No buyback to judge | [1] |
| FY2024 | 0.0 | EUR | 0 | N/A | 269.9m → 287.4m (+6.5%) | No — SBC expense €171.1m, zero offset | Not applicable | No buyback to judge | [1] |
| FY2025 | 0.0 | EUR | 0 | N/A | 287.4m → 298.2m (+3.8%) | No — SBC expense €224.1m, zero offset | Not applicable | No buyback to judge | [1] |
| **Cumulative FY2021–2025** | **0.0** | EUR | 0 | N/A | **199.3m → 298.2m, +49.6%** | **No, in any year** | **None exists** | **No group-level buyback program existed at any point in the window** | [1] |

This module runs BEFORE valuation, so buyback timing is **not** judged against intrinsic/fair value here — the valuation module has not run at the time of this report, so "Intrinsic-value discipline not assessable from this module" for the (non-existent) equity buybacks.

**A distinct, real capital-structure action must not be confused with an equity buyback.** In 2024–2025 DHER repurchased and cancelled roughly **€452m (2024) and €895.9m (2025)** face value of its own **convertible bonds** (debt instruments, not common shares) through tender offers and private/open-market purchases — €409.2m + €33.2m + €9.5m in 2024, and €23.5m + €537.5m + €334.9m in the Feb-2025 tender [FY24 Annual Report, Corporate Financing section, p.94–95]. These purchases were funded largely by new term-loan and add-on-facility issuance, not by discretionary FCF, and their purpose was maturity extension and (for the converts specifically) removing future conversion overhang before it dilutes shareholders — a modestly shareholder-protective act, but it is debt-liability management, not a capital-return program, and it does not appear as "Buybacks" in Section 1's uses-of-capital table (it is embedded in the "Debt repaid" line). Calling this a "buyback" without the distinction would overstate shareholder-return discipline that did not occur.

**Net effect for a holder of DHER common stock:** shares outstanding rose 49.6% over five years (199.3m → 298.2m) with **zero** offsetting mechanism at the parent level. Talabat (an 80%-owned subsidiary) separately announced its own subsidiary-level buyback of Talabat shares, into which DHER (as majority owner) explicitly declined to sell [Delivery Hero SE, 2025 Earnings Call transcript, 26-Mar-2026, p.11] — that is Talabat managing its own share count, not DHER managing DHER's, and does not change the parent-level dilution finding above. **RF-CAP-002 (buybacks not reducing share count) does not technically trigger** because there were no buybacks to test — the substantive concern (uncompensated dilution) is captured directly in the Per-Share Value Creation score (Section 4 below and the scorecard).

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Payout ratio | 0% in every year FY2020–FY2025 (no dividend declared; net loss in every year, from −€782.9m (FY2025) to −€3,008.4m (FY2022)) | Cash Flow tab, "Total Dividends Paid" (blank); Cash Flow tab, "Net Income" [1] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | Not applicable — zero dividends means there is nothing to cover; a coverage ratio would be undefined/meaningless against a €0 numerator | [1] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital) | **Not meaningfully computable from this pool.** Reported (statutory) EBIT improved by €1,752.2m from FY2021 (−€1,658.5m) to FY2025 (+€93.7m) [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md` §1], while a standard invested-capital proxy (Total Debt + Total Equity − Cash) **fell** from €7,515.1m (FY2021: debt €4,518.0m + equity €5,443.8m − cash €2,446.7m) to €4,306.6m (FY2025: debt €4,625.5m + equity €1,793.8m − cash €2,112.7m) [Balance Sheet tab, computed]. Dividing an improving EBIT by a *shrinking* capital base is mechanically nonsensical here: the capital base shrank mainly because €2,054.0m of goodwill was **written off** (Section 2) and cumulative net losses drove Retained Earnings from −€3,323.6m (FY2021) to −€11,059.3m (FY2025) — that is capital destroyed, not capital returned or efficiently redeployed. This agent declines to publish a numeric incremental-ROIC figure that would misleadingly imply an improving return on a capital base that was substantially impaired, not shrunk by discipline. | Balance Sheet tab [1]; `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` §1 |
| Capex intensity (organic reinvestment) | Capex stayed low and roughly flat relative to revenue growth — €169.0m (FY2020) → €171.0m (FY2025) on a PP&E-only basis, or €320.9m (FY2021) → €325.8m (FY2025) on the broader PP&E+intangibles basis — against revenue growing from €2.47bn to €14.06bn (5.7x); capex has run below D&A (€360.9m, FY2025) in every year, consistent with an asset-light marketplace model rather than heavy organic reinvestment | Cash Flow tab [1]; `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` §1 |
| Book value per share | €22.32 (FY2021) → €5.50 (FY2025), a 75.4% decline, despite the share count nearly doubling in the denominator and equity being repeatedly diluted by losses | Balance Sheet tab, "Book Value/Share" [1] |
| Tangible book value per share | **Negative in every year FY2021–FY2025**: −€6.45 (FY2021) → −€16.19 (FY2022) → −€17.79 (FY2023) → −€11.84 (FY2024) → −€11.84 (FY2025, CIQ-reported figure repeats FY2024's value — flagged as a possible data-vendor carry-forward, not independently verified against the audited FY2025 balance sheet, which is not in the pool as an audited filing) | Balance Sheet tab, "Tangible Book Value/Share" [1] |

The organic-reinvestment story is genuinely disciplined on the spending side (low, capex below D&A, asset-light) but the returns side cannot be honestly quantified as an improving incremental ROIC given how much of the FY2021 capital base was subsequently written off rather than earning a return; the book-value and tangible-book-value trends are the more honest per-share read, and both point the same direction — down, sharply, throughout the window.

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Over FY2021–FY2025, Delivery Hero spent money it did not have from operations (cumulative CFO −€892.9m) on a mega-deal acquisition program (Woowa ~$4.0bn, Glovo €2.3bn headline) that has since absorbed €2,054.0m of cumulative goodwill impairment — direct, audited proof the company overpaid — while share count grew 49.6% with zero offsetting buyback in any year, driving book value per share down 75.4% and tangible book value per share negative in every single year of the window. The best use of capital in the period was the Talabat 20% IPO placement (Dec-2024, ~€1.8bn realized while retaining an 80% controlling stake) — a genuine, market-tested value-recycling event, alongside a defensible zero-dividend policy given persistent net losses and disciplined, below-D&A capex; the worst use was the acquisition program itself, whose opportunity cost extended beyond the write-downs to a forced Korean-unit divestiture (Woowa antitrust condition) and an inherited, unresolved up-to-€770m Spain rider-classification legal exposure now sitting alongside a subsidiary-level going-concern note. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is that a five-year record of serial, debt-and-stock-funded M&A plus uncompensated dilution left the company financially stretched enough that, as of this report's date, its reference shareholder has agreed to tender the entire company to Uber rather than continue standalone — the clearest evidence available that per-share value was not the organizing principle of the last five years' capital deployment. **Verdict: value-destructive / size-driven.**

| Balance-sheet prudence | 5 | 10 | Net debt/Adj. EBITDA moderate (~2.7-2.8x) and a new 2032 term loan reduces near-term refinancing risk, but net debt rose again in FY2025 after a FY2024 low (Finding 02-022), and a subsidiary-level going-concern emphasis-of-matter plus an unrecognized up-to-€770m contingent liability sit outside the leverage ratio entirely |
| Per-share value creation | 2 | 10 | Book value per share fell 75.4% (Finding 02-020) and tangible book value per share was negative in every year of the window (Finding 02-021) — the single clearest, most direct per-share value-destruction signal in this scorecard |
| **Total** | **40** | **100** | Weak band (21-40) per `MODULE_RULES.md` Scoring Rules. Slightly below `business-model/11`'s 42/100 quick-read score — this deep-dive weighs the book-value/tangible-book-value per-share destruction (Findings 02-020, 02-021) more heavily than the quick-read did, consistent with this module superseding that read per `MODULE_RULES.md` |

**Score Cap Rules applied:** Serial-acquirer pattern (RF-CAP-004, Finding 02-009) — Capital Allocation Score capped at max 50 per `MODULE_RULES.md`; actual score (40) sits below that cap, so the cap is confirmed but not the binding constraint — the underlying evidence independently supports a sub-50 score.
