## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — DHER

**Context carried from `00_governance-data-triage.md` and `01_management-and-track-record.md`:** Delivery Hero SE is currently the subject of a live, announced acquisition offer from Uber Technologies (M&A call held Jul 16, 2026); reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers is "irrevocably committed to tender its stake" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE - M&A Call, Jul 16 2026, p.4]. `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` also triggered a hard disqualifier (auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level going-concern note on Glovo Spain, €440–770m contingent social-security liability). This agent does not re-adjudicate that lock, but it bears on how the compensation read below should be weighted downstream. The compensation disclosure below is drawn from the FY2024 Annual Report's Compensation Report (Section 162 AktG, KPMG-audited) — the German proxy-equivalent — published Apr 25, 2025 and therefore ~15.6 months old at report date; no FY2025 Compensation Report is in the pool, so the FY2024 structure and figures below are the most current audited comp data available, and the live change-of-control provisions triggered by the pending Uber deal (Section 3 below) are read against that FY2024-dated contractual language.

## 1. Compensation Structure

Reporting currency: EUR. Reporting period: financial year 2024 (Jan 1 – Dec 31, 2024), as disclosed in the Compensation Report 2024 within the FY2024 Annual Report (published Apr 25, 2025). Figures below are **target** compensation (100% target achievement) unless stated otherwise — actual payouts depend on target achievement determined after each plan's performance/waiting period, so FY2024 STI/LTI grants will not be known as "final" numbers of shares until 2026 (STI, 2-year wait) and 2028 (LTI, 4-year wait).

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus (STI, target) | Long-Term Incentive (LTI, target) | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO — Niklas Östberg | €500k (10%) | €1,500k (29%) | €3,175k (61%) — total target comp €5,176k (2023: €4,525k, of which LTIP was 88%) | 90% of target comp is variable ("at risk"); STI settles in cash or shares after a 2-yr wait (Supervisory Board "intends to settle in shares"), LTI in cash or shares after a 4-yr wait (same stated intent) — effectively equity-heavy | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, "Total Target Compensation of the Management Board" table, p.45 |
| CFO — Emmanuel Thomassin (to Jun 30, 2024) | €500k (19%) target basis | €600k (23%) target basis | €1,550k (58%) target basis — total target comp €2,650k (2023: €2,300k, of which LTIP was 80%) | Actual FY2024 grants were pro-rated to his Apr 1–Jun 30, 2024 vesting period: €0.2m STI / €0.4m LTI virtual-share grants, not the full-year target figures shown | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.45, p.53 (pro-ration footnote) |
| CFO — Marie-Anne Popp (interim CFO from Jul 1, 2024; permanent CFO & Management Board member from Jan 2025) | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data — she was not yet a formally appointed Management Board member during FY2024, so no FY2024 target-compensation row is disclosed for her in the Compensation Report; her comp structure will first appear in the FY2025 Compensation Report, not in this pool | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(a), p.207 (appointment fact only) |

**IFRS accounting-basis figure — do not conflate with the table above.** Note 10(c) ("Key Management Personnel Transactions," IAS 24 basis, combining Management Board *and* Supervisory Board) states short-term employee benefits of €3.9m and share-based-compensation expense of €2.9m for 2024, and separately states "the total remuneration of the Management Board amounted to €2.7 million (previous year: €1.4 million)" — a cash/short-term-benefits-scoped figure on a different accounting basis (expense recognition timing, Management-Board-only) than the €5,176k (CEO) / €2,650k (CFO) DCGK-mandated *target* compensation table above. Both figures are cited to their own source and should not be substituted for one another [FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(c), p.207].

Long-term incentive plan design changed starting FY2024. From FY2018–FY2023, the Management Board's variable long-term pay was a Long-Term Incentive Plan (LTIP) of stock options tied to a single performance test: revenue CAGR ≥20% over a four-year performance period, pass/fail (100% or 0%, no interpolation) [FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.50–53]. Tranches granted 2018–2023 are still outstanding and vesting through exercise windows extending to 2029 [FY24 Annual Report, p.52].

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus (STI, since FY2024) | Growth metric — Supervisory Board selects ONE from: Revenue, GMV, or Gross Profit | Combined with profitability metric: 70–75% of STI | **Size-based** — all three growth-leg options (revenue, GMV, gross profit) are absolute size measures with no per-share or dilution adjustment | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.47–48 |
| Annual bonus (STI, since FY2024) | Profitability metric — Supervisory Board selects ONE from: adj. EBITDA, adj. EBITDA-Margin, or Free Cash Flow | Combined with growth metric: 70–75% of STI | **Mixed** — adj. EBITDA and Free Cash Flow are absolute (size-adjacent); adj. EBITDA-Margin is a ratio, closer to a returns-based metric. The specific metric(s) the Supervisory Board actually selected for FY2024 is **not disclosed** in the extracted text — a genuine clarity gap | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.47–48 |
| Annual bonus (STI, since FY2024) | ESG targets (environment/social/governance catalogue — e.g. rider welfare, CO2, "best in class" governance, data security) | 25–30% of STI | **Neither per-share nor size** — qualitative/non-financial; before FY2024 the STI bonus was based exclusively on ESG targets, i.e. 100% non-financial | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.47–48 |
| Long-Term Incentive (LTI, since FY2024) | Relative Total Shareholder Return (Delivery Hero TSR vs. MSCI World Consumer Services Index), 4-yr performance period | 70% of LTI | **Per-share/returns-based** — TSR is a direct per-share, market-priced, relative-performance metric; this is the strongest alignment element in the whole structure | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.49–50 |
| Long-Term Incentive (LTI, since FY2024) | Cumulative operating cash flow over the 4-yr performance period | 30% of LTI | **Size-based (absolute)** — cumulative operating cash flow is a total-euro figure, not scaled per share or per unit of capital employed | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.49–50 |
| Legacy LTIP (FY2018–FY2023 grants, still vesting through 2029) | Revenue CAGR over a 4-yr performance period, target ≥20%, binary pass/fail (100% or 0%) | 100% of the legacy LTIP's performance test | **Purely size-based** — a raw top-line growth hurdle with no profitability, cash, or per-share condition attached; every disclosed tranche cleared it comfortably (e.g. Tranche 2018 actual CAGR 82% vs. 20% target; Tranche 2019 90%; Tranche 2020 80%) during years in which the Group posted net losses of €2,304.7m (FY2023) and €881.7m (FY2024) and issued €2,138.8m of new capital contributions in FY2024 alone | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.52–53; FY24 Annual Report, Consolidated Statement of Profit or Loss, p.145; Consolidated Statement of Changes in Equity, p.146 |

**Read on the metrics.** The FY2024 redesign is a real, evidenced improvement over the pre-2024 regime: TSR at 70% of the LTI is a genuine per-share metric, and malus/clawback provisions were extended to cover the STI/LTI for the first time in FY2024 (see Section 3). But the STI's growth leg remains a menu of absolute-size options, the exact FY2024 selection is not disclosed in this filing, and a large stock of legacy options — granted purely for hitting a 20% revenue-CAGR bar during the company's heaviest loss-making and most dilutive years — continues to vest through 2029. The new system has one year of history; it has not yet been tested through a full four-year LTI cycle.

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| CEO total pay (latest, target basis) | €5,176k target total compensation for FY2024 (up from €4,525k in FY2023, +14%); actual FY2024 grants to Östberg were €1.5m of STI virtual shares and €3.2m of LTI virtual shares (~€4.7m), close to target | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.45, p.52 |
| Pay vs peers (web-sourced, unverified) | Just Eat Takeaway's CEO Jitse Groen received total compensation of approximately €1.6m for FY2023 (base ~€670k) — a much smaller figure than Östberg's €4.5–5.2m target range, though Just Eat Takeaway is a materially smaller company by revenue than Delivery Hero (~€12.3bn FY2024 revenue). No like-for-like, size-adjusted peer-comp benchmarking data exists in the pool; this single data point is directional only, not a rigorous comparison | Web: simplywall.st, retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified, estimate, no error margin stated) |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | Positive: Adjusted EBITDA — a candidate STI profitability metric — was guided and delivered within 1–2 percentage points in each of FY2023, FY2024, and FY2025 (`01_management-and-track-record.md` §4, Findings 01-008–01-010). Negative: Free Cash Flow, the other candidate STI profitability metric, swung from a large headline "beat" in FY2024 (flattered by one-off items) to a €366m headline miss in FY2025 (`01_management-and-track-record.md` §4, Finding 01-012); the Street missed EPS estimates in every one of the last five fiscal years (Finding 01-014). Because the exact FY2024 STI metric selection is undisclosed, the specific linkage between pay outcomes and these results cannot be traced with precision from this filing alone | `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4; FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.47–48 |
| Say-on-pay support (%) | The FY2023 Compensation Report was approved by the Annual General Meeting on June 19, 2024, with 93.05% of votes cast — high shareholder support, no material say-on-pay opposition signal for that year. The FY2024 Compensation Report vote is scheduled for the AGM of June 18, 2025 (i.e., after this Annual Report's Apr 25, 2025 publication date) — its outcome is **not in the pool** | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.40 |
| Structural deviation from the approved compensation system | The Supervisory Board temporarily raised COO Pieter-Jan Vandepitte's base salary by €400,000 (an in-year deviation from the "target total remuneration set before the start of the financial year" rule) to secure him as interim CEO of the South Korean subsidiary Woowa Brothers during an unplanned local-leadership gap. This pushed his actual fixed/variable ratio to 29.51% fixed / 50.82% LTI / 19.67% STI, outside the approved 10–25% / 55–65% / 20–30% bands. Transparently disclosed and narrowly scoped, but it shows the Supervisory Board is willing to deviate from its own AGM-approved compensation system when operationally convenient | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.59–60 |
| Change-of-control acceleration (live relevance given the pending Uber offer) | Management Board members may resign within three months of a change of control; on such resignation, **all outstanding STI/LTI incentive instruments become fully vested immediately, irrespective of vesting periods or cliff provisions**. A departing member is also entitled to compensation of up to two years' total pay (capped at the remaining service-agreement term). No malus/clawback carve-out or performance re-test applies at that point — the acceleration is unconditional on resignation following a change of control. This is a live, material fact given the Uber tender offer process disclosed in `01_management-and-track-record.md` and `00_governance-data-triage.md`: it creates a direct personal financial incentive for Management Board members to see a change-of-control transaction complete, separate from — and not conditioned on — the price achieved for all shareholders | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.39–40 |
| Malus and clawback | Extended for the first time in FY2024 to cover both the STI and LTI (previously only the legacy LTIP and STI bonus were covered), for material compliance breaches or an incorrect consolidated financial statement. No malus/clawback was triggered in FY2024 | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.42, p.59 |
| Maximum compensation cap | CEO: €12,000,000; ordinary Management Board members: €9,000,000 (total compensation cap, all components, per service-agreement year) — a transparent ceiling, well above what was actually paid/granted in FY2024 | FY24 Annual Report, Compensation Report 2024, p.42–43 |
| Dilution context (company-wide, not exec-only) | Group-wide share-based-compensation expense was €86.1m (FY2020), peaked at €325.9m (FY2022), fell to €171.1m (FY2024), and rose to €224.1m (FY2025) — a scale indicator of how much of the equity-heavy incentive structure (across all employees, not just the Management Board) has translated into share-count dilution over time, alongside €2,138.8m of fresh capital contributions raised in FY2024 alone | Delivery-Hero-SE-XTRA-DHER-Financials.xls, Income Statement tab; FY24 Annual Report, Consolidated Statement of Changes in Equity, p.146 |

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

The FY2024 redesign is a real, cited improvement: the Long-Term Incentive now weights relative Total Shareholder Return at 70% — a genuine per-share, market-tested metric — and malus/clawback was extended to cover both the STI and LTI for the first time. Set against that: the STI's growth leg is still chosen from three absolute-size options (revenue, GMV, gross profit) with the FY2024 selection undisclosed in this filing, a large book of legacy stock options — granted purely for clearing a 20% revenue-CAGR bar with no profitability or per-share test, comfortably beaten every year through FY2023 while the Group posted billions of euros in net losses and heavy share issuance — continues vesting through 2029, and the live Uber change-of-control provisions hand Management Board members full, unconditional vesting of all unvested equity on resignation, a direct personal incentive to see a deal close that is not tied to the per-share price achieved for other shareholders. The single biggest incentive signal is this split character: a genuinely well-designed new framework, only one year old and untested through a full vesting cycle, layered on top of a multi-year legacy of pure growth-for-growth's-sake option grants that are still working their way through the share count. **Conclusion: mixed.**

| Returns / per-share metrics | 12 | 25 | LTI's 70% relative-TSR weight is a genuine per-share metric (Finding 03-007), but the STI growth leg is menu-limited to absolute-size options with the FY2024 choice undisclosed (03-004), and a large stock of legacy options rewarded pure revenue-CAGR growth with no per-share test (03-009, 03-010) |

| **Total** | **57** | **100** | Mixed band (41–60). ## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md

_Source: `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`_

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — DHER

**Context note (carried from `00_governance-data-triage.md` and `01_management-and-track-record.md`).** Delivery Hero SE is, as of this report's date, the subject of a live, announced acquisition offer from Uber Technologies (M&A call held Jul 16, 2026; expected close H2 2027). Uber states it already has "economic exposure to 37% of Delivery Hero" and that reference shareholder Prosus (via Naspers Limited / MIH Food Holdings B.V.) has "irrevocably committed to tender its stake, which brings our economic ownership position to over 50% following a successful offer" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE — M&A Call, Jul 16, 2026, p.4/7]. This is a live corporate-action overlay, not a steady-state ownership picture — the read below separates the static FY2024 pool disclosure from this in-progress control-structure event. Separately, `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` found no promoter/insider share-pledging disclosure anywhere in the pool, and confirmed Naspers/MIH is the only >10%-voting-rights holder disclosed, with no controlling promoter.

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO / founder (Niklas Östberg, individually) | Not itemized separately in the pool | Ordinary registered shares (single class) | Not disclosed at individual level in this pool's extracted text | FY24 Annual Report, p.32–33 (no individual beneficial-ownership table below the >10% threshold) |
| Other insiders (aggregate — Management Board: Östberg, Vandepitte, Thomassin/Popp) | **0.38%** (1,098,191 shares / 287,385,940 total subscribed shares, at Dec 31, 2024) | Ordinary registered shares (single class) | **Granted** — the pool's compensation notes describe Management Board equity exclusively via LTIP virtual-share tranches (Performance Shares), STI virtual shares, and Hero Grant RSUs (30-day-average-price-derived, vesting over 12–48 months); no disclosure of an open-market cash purchase by any Management Board member appears in the extracted text | FY24 Annual Report, "Shareholdings exceeding 10% of voting rights" section (restricted-voting-rights disclosure), p.32; Compensation Report LTIP/STI/Hero Grant detail, pp.~2160–2521, 9750–9865 |
| Largest institutional / strategic holder | **>10% of voting rights** at Dec 31, 2024 (exact % not itemized in the pool's extracted text) — Naspers Limited, via MIH Food Holdings B.V. (part of Prosus N.V.) | Ordinary registered shares (single class) | Bought — a long-standing strategic/financial investment stake, not company-granted | FY24 Annual Report, "Shareholdings exceeding 10% of voting rights," p.32–33 |
| Acquirer building a pre-completion stake | **37%** economic exposure to DHER as of Jul 16, 2026 (pool-sourced, current control-structure event, not a static FY2024 disclosure) — Uber Technologies, Inc. | Not specified in the pool (transcript does not disaggregate direct shares vs derivative/economic exposure) | Bought — accumulated ahead of/alongside the announced acquisition offer | Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE — M&A Call, Jul 16, 2026, p.7 |
| Promoter / controlling bloc | **None** — the company states "there are no different share classes... no shares with special rights conferring powers of control," and no single holder is disclosed at a majority-control level in the pool as of report date | N/A | N/A | FY24 Annual Report, p.32 |

**Total insider (Management Board) ownership: 0.38%** of subscribed capital, per the pool's only disclosed figure (the 1,098,191-share Section-136-AktG voting-rights restriction, as of Dec 31, 2024). This is thin skin-in-the-game for a founder-CEO narrative, and it is entirely compensation-derived rather than bought — see the Bought-vs-Granted read below. The Supervisory Board's individual or aggregate shareholding is not disclosed anywhere in the pool's extracted text; that is a genuine gap, not an assumed zero.

**Web-sourced ownership context (unverified, not from a pool filing — labeled per source hierarchy; per the F20 gating rule these figures are reported as context only and do not by themselves support any red flag).** Public reporting indicates Naspers/Prosus held roughly 26.5% of DHER before 2026, and was required by European Commission conditions attached to Prosus's own acquisition of Just Eat Takeaway.com to cut that stake; on Apr 17, 2026 Prosus disposed of 13,582,342 shares (~4.5% of issued capital) to Uber, and on May 11, 2026 disposed of a further 15,188,284 shares (~5%) to Aspex Management at €22.00/share, leaving Prosus at ~16.8% — the stake it has now irrevocably committed to tender into Uber's offer, reported at €41.50/share cash (~$14.8bn deal value) [Web: Prosus press releases ("Prosus sells 5% interest in Delivery Hero to Aspex Management"; "Prosus gives irrevocable undertaking to sell residual stake in Delivery Hero to Uber"), Reuters (via TradingView), Bloomberg, retrieved 2026-08-12, unverified]. None of these percentages or prices appear in the pool; they are reported here as labeled web context, consistent with the partial-data rule, and are excluded from any scored/red-flagged finding below.

## 2. Insider Transactions (last 12 months)

| Insider | Buy / Sell | Amount | Date | Notes (10b5-1?) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| No PDMR (Persons Discharging Managerial Responsibilities) buy/sell transaction is reproduced in the pool's extracted text | — | — | — | The Annual Report references the MAR Article 19(11) closed-period obligation and states the company publishes PDMR share transactions on its own website, but no transaction table (dates/shares/prices) is present in this pool | FY24 Annual Report, p.32 |

**Net insider activity: Insufficient Data.** This module's pool contains no PDMR (Form-4-equivalent) transaction log — only the disclosure that such an obligation exists and that transactions are published on the company's website (not reproduced here). Per the module's F20 red-flag gating rule, this absence caps the "Recent insider behavior" score component to low confidence / insufficient data; it is not scored as neutral or as a clean record.

**Distinct from insider transactions: institutional block trades.** The ownership-percentage changes described above (Prosus's April/May 2026 disposals to Uber and Aspex Management, and Uber's stake-build to 37% economic exposure) are major-shareholder / voting-rights-notification events (the German WpHG regime), not PDMR insider dealing by company officers — Naspers/Prosus, Aspex Management, and Uber are not "persons discharging managerial responsibilities" at Delivery Hero. They are reported in Section 4 (Control Structure) and Section 4A (Market Conduct), not conflated with the insider-transaction read here.

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| No insider or strategic-holder share pledge is disclosed | 0 | 0% | FY24 Annual Report (no pledge disclosure found); confirmed by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, which states "no promoter/insider share-pledge disclosure found anywhere in the pool" |

**No pledging disclosed.** The only "pledge" language anywhere in the pool relates to corporate financing — €11.6m of a €48.3m facility pledged as security, and intercompany receivables/bank accounts pledged under the Korean Won Term Facility [FY24 Annual Report, Note F, p.~8750, p.~9140] — which is company-level loan collateral, not an insider or strategic-holder share pledge. This is a clean finding, not a gap: Germany's WpHG/GCGC regime carries no promoter-pledge disclosure requirement, and none of the pool's sources reference any encumbrance on the Naspers/Prosus, Management Board, or Supervisory Board holdings.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | None — single share class, one vote per share, no shares with special control rights | FY24 Annual Report, p.32: "There are no different share classes... no shares with special rights conferring powers of control" |
| Controlled-company status | Not a controlled company as of the FY2024 disclosure (no holder above the disclosed >10% threshold reaches majority control). This is changing in real time: Uber states current economic exposure of 37% of DHER, and Prosus's irrevocable tender commitment would bring Uber's economic ownership "to over 50% following a successful offer" | Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE — M&A Call, Jul 16, 2026, p.7 |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | Prosus previously held one Supervisory Board seat (Roger Rabalais, "Operating partner at Prosus Group," service period May 1–Dec 31, 2024); no successor Prosus-nominated director is named in the pool's FY2024 disclosure, so as of report date the pool shows this bloc without a standing board seat. Web-sourced (unverified) context: activist fund Aspex Management built a stake to roughly 15% by May 2026 and publicly pressured the company to replace CEO Östberg and divest entire regions | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(b), p.207 (pool); Web: Reuters (via TradingView/sahmcapital), ESM Magazine, retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified) |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | **Not applicable as of report date** — no single controlling owner exists in the pool's disclosed ownership structure; the largest disclosed holder (Naspers/Prosus) held a minority (>10%) stake and is now exiting entirely via a third-party cash acquisition, not exercising standing control. See the unaligned-owner discussion below for the forward-looking risk this creates if the tender offer completes without a full squeeze-out. | Inference from Sections 1 and 4 above — not from filings |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6).** None of the three disqualifying structures (government control; listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent; sprawling unrelated conglomerate) applies to Delivery Hero SE as of the report date — ownership is dispersed, single-class, and the largest strategic holder (Naspers/Prosus) never exercised majority control. **RF-OWN-004 is not triggered.** There is, however, a genuine forward risk worth flagging for any investor holding through the deal: if Uber's tender offer succeeds but falls short of the threshold needed for a full squeeze-out/delisting under German takeover law, remaining minority DHER shareholders would sit under a majority-owner parent (Uber) whose objective — maximizing value for Uber shareholders through integration synergies (management guided to "$1.2 billion" of run-rate synergies within 18 months of close and "high single-digit percentage" non-GAAP EPS accretion by year 3 [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE — M&A Call, Jul 16, 2026, p.5]) — would not necessarily be to maximize DHER's own standalone per-share value. This is a live, deal-structure-dependent scenario, not a current control abuse, and is noted as a watch item rather than a scored red flag.

| Insider (PDMR) buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | Not assessable — no PDMR transaction log is in the pool (Section 2) | Insufficient Data |
| Unusual price / volume before announcement | Not assessed from pool data (trading/volume series sit outside this module's pool sources). Web-sourced (unverified) context: Aspex Management's May 11, 2026 purchase of a 5% Prosus stake was reported at €22.00/share — "a 10% premium to Delivery Hero's closing price... and about 22% above the stock's 30-day volume-weighted average" — roughly two months before Uber's reported €41.50/share cash offer (Jul 16, 2026), a gap of close to 90% between the two prices | Web: Reuters (via sahmcapital/TradingView), retrieved 2026-08-12 (unverified) — reported as context only; per the F20 gating rule this does NOT trigger RF-MKT-001/002, since it is not sourced to a pool filing |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | Not evidenced in the pool | Insufficient Data |
| Institutional (major-holder) trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Naspers/Prosus — the only >10% holder disclosed in the pool at FY2024 year-end — has fully exited its position across 2026: two disposals (to Uber and to Aspex Management, web-sourced) reduced its stake before it irrevocably committed to tender the remainder into Uber's offer. This reads as a regulatory-compelled divestiture (EU antitrust conditions on Prosus's own Just Eat Takeaway acquisition, per web reporting) followed by a friendly cash exit at a premium, not a reference shareholder fleeing a deteriorating outlook — but it is nonetheless a complete exit by the company's largest disclosed shareholder within a single year | Pool: FY24 Annual Report, p.32–33 (>10% threshold); Uber-DHER M&A Call, p.4 (tender commitment). Web (unverified): specific percentages, prices, and the antitrust-driven rationale, retrieved 2026-08-12 |

**Read on market conduct.** No insider (PDMR) selling ahead of weak results, and no insider buying ahead of good news, can be evidenced from the pool — the data simply is not there (Insufficient Data, not a clean bill of health). The one timing pattern visible at all — Aspex's ~€22/share purchase roughly two months ahead of a ~€41.50/share takeover offer — involves an outside activist fund, not a company insider, is entirely web-sourced, and per the F20 gating rule does not support a red flag; it is noted only as a follow-up item for a future pool refresh (PDMR log, exact Naspers/Aspex percentages and dates) that would let this read be made on primary evidence instead of unverified web coverage.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Skin in the game inside the company is thin and entirely compensation-derived: the whole Management Board together held 0.38% of the company at 2024 year-end (1,098,191 of 287,385,940 shares), built through LTIP, STI, and Hero Grant equity awards rather than open-market purchase — so the founder-CEO narrative rests on tenure, not on money Niklas Östberg himself put at risk buying stock. There is no pledging of insider or strategic-holder shares, and no dual-class or super-voting structure — on those two points, minority holders are protected. The dominant fact right now is not steady-state ownership at all: the pool shows Uber Technologies already holding economic exposure to 37% of Delivery Hero and reference shareholder Naspers/Prosus irrevocably committed to tender its remaining stake, pushing Uber's position "over 50%" if the offer succeeds — a live control-structure event, not a governance signal to score on its own terms. The single biggest gap for this read is data, not conduct: no PDMR (insider) transaction log sits in the pool, so recent insider buying/selling cannot be verified from primary sources, and the richer, more concerning story visible only in unverified web coverage — an activist fund building to ~15% and pressuring the founder-CEO to step down, shortly before a premium takeover bid emerged — has to be flagged as context only, not evidence, until a pool-sourced filing confirms it.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — DHER

**Context carried from `management-governance/00_governance-data-triage.md` and `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`.** Delivery Hero SE (Frankfurt Prime Standard, XTRA:DHER; German Societas Europaea under the two-tier board system — a Management Board that runs the company and a Supervisory Board that appoints and oversees it) is, as of report date, the subject of a live, announced acquisition offer from Uber Technologies (M&A call, Jul 16, 2026), with reference shareholder Prosus/Naspers "irrevocably committed to tender its stake," which Uber says "brings our economic ownership position to over 50% following a successful offer... we will evaluate the most appropriate ownership structure based on the outcome of the tender offer and the options available under German corporate law" [Uber Technologies, Inc., Delivery Hero SE - M&A Call, Jul 16 2026, p.8]. That last phrase is a direct signal that a post-closing squeeze-out or domination agreement — mechanisms that can compress or extinguish remaining minority holders' economic position under German law — is under consideration; this is read as the current, forward-looking centre of gravity for minority-shareholder protection, layered on top of the historical governance record below. Separately, `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` triggered a hard disqualifier (an auditor-flagged, subsidiary-level "Material Uncertainty about the Ability of Subsidiaries to Continue as a Going Concern" tied to Glovo Spain's rider-classification investigation, contingent liability €440–770 million, Group opinion unmodified) [FY24 Annual Report, Independent Auditor's Report, p.222–223]. Per MODULE_RULES.md Disqualifier Deference, this agent reports that fact verbatim and does not re-adjudicate it; it is not itself a board-structure or shareholder-rights defect, but the downstream synthesis must apply the Governance-risk floor and cap the overall stewardship verdict at no better than "Serious governance concerns" regardless of the board-quality read below.

**Partial-data cap applied (per `00_governance-data-triage.md`).** No full beneficial-ownership table and no PDMR (director/officer) buy-sell transaction log are in the pool — only the sole >10%-of-voting-rights WpHG notification (Naspers/MIH Food Holdings) is reproduced in the filing. Per MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules ("No ownership / insider-transaction data"), **Shareholder friendliness is capped at 60/100** regardless of the raw component scores below.

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board structure | German two-tier system: a **Management Board** (runs the company) and a **Supervisory Board** (Aufsichtsrat — appoints, monitors, and advises the Management Board). This is the local equivalent of a single US-style board, not a US board; the "independence" question is asked of the Supervisory Board, not of a unified board | FY24 Annual Report, p.22 |
| Supervisory Board size | 8 members: 4 shareholder representatives + 4 employee representatives (elected by the SE Works Council, a legally mandated German/EU codetermination structure, not a governance choice by the company) | FY24 Annual Report, p.12 |
| Independent directors (%) | Company-stated target: "more than half of the shareholder representatives... shall be independent of the Company and the Management Board" [FY24 Annual Report, p.25] — i.e., independence is measured against the 4 shareholder seats, not the full 8-seat board (the 4 employee seats are not assessed for "independence" under the GCGC framework at all). Only one shareholder representative, Roger Rabalais, is individually confirmed "independent" by name in the text reviewed [FY24 Annual Report, p.29]; a board-wide statement says "all members confirmed their independence from the Company and the Management Board" following an in-depth Nov 5, 2024 discussion, but the qualification-matrix "Independence" row's individual marks were not extractable as text (a table/graphic-mark limitation, not a disclosure gap) [FY24 Annual Report, p.14, p.26] | FY24 Annual Report, p.14, p.25–26, p.29 |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes, structurally guaranteed by the two-tier system: Niklas Östberg is Chair of the Management Board (CEO); Kristin Skogen Lund is Chair of the Supervisory Board — two different people, and German law bars any overlap between the roles while in office | FY24 Annual Report, p.13, p.17 |
| Audit / Comp / Nominating committee independence | **Audit Committee** (3 members: Roger Rabalais [Chair], Dr Martin Enderle [Deputy Chair], Isabel Poscherstnikov [employee representative]) — "consists of two independent members pursuant to Sections 100(5) and 107(4) AktG" [FY24 Annual Report, p.14]. GCGC recommends the Audit Committee chair be independent "from controlling shareholders" specifically; Roger Rabalais, the Chair, is disclosed elsewhere in the same filing as "Operating partner at Prosus Group" [FY24 Annual Report, p.207] — Prosus (via Naspers/MIH Food Holdings) is the Company's sole disclosed >10%-voting-rights holder and, as of the 2026 Uber deal, the shareholder "irrevocably committed to tender." Prosus/Naspers is not a controlling shareholder in the majority-ownership sense, so this does not breach the letter of the GCGC recommendation, but an Audit Committee chaired by the reference shareholder's own operating partner is a real independence-quality question, not a clean pass. Rabalais departed the Supervisory Board effective Dec 31, 2024, so this specific overlap is historical, not current. **Nomination Committee** (3 members, all shareholder representatives, per design) and **Remuneration Committee** (4 members) are chaired by Kristin Skogen Lund, independently confirmed as Chair of the Supervisory Board | FY24 Annual Report, p.14, p.29–30, p.207 |

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | Skewed young by a large FY2024 refresh: Dr Martin Enderle has served since 2017 (~7–9 years); Gabriella Ardbo Engarås and Nils Engvall since 2020; Dimitrios Tsaousis since 2021 (with a brief gap); Kristin Skogen Lund, Roger Rabalais (departed), Scott Ferguson, and Isabel Poscherstnikov all joined in 2024. Weighted average tenure across the 8-member board at Dec 31 2024 is roughly 2.5–3 years | FY24 Annual Report, p.25 (individual "first appointment" dates in the qualification matrix) |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | No overboarding evidenced among directors serving at report date: Scott Ferguson (Sachem Head Capital founder) lists no other directorships in the filing; Dr Martin Enderle sits on a handful of other boards (Egmont Foundation, Boats Group LLC) but not an excessive count. One departed director, Patrick Kolek (left Apr 30, 2024), held five-plus concurrent board/advisory seats (Skillsoft Corp Chair, NDH LLP, Toposware, Granite Comfort, Boats Group CEO) — a genuine overboarding signal, but for a director no longer on the board. Term/age limits exist: a 12-year Supervisory Board term limit and a 70-year age limit apply, "from which, however, deviations can be made in justified individual cases" — a soft, not hard, cap | FY24 Annual Report, p.207, p.25 |
| Recent refreshment | Substantial: **5 of 8 seats changed within calendar-2024** (a new Chair, a departed Deputy Chair, two other departures, two new arrivals — cross-referenced from `management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md`, Finding 01-006), plus the board's own size increased from 6 to 8 members by AGM resolution on June 19, 2024. This is real refreshment, but concentrated enough in one year to be a continuity risk rather than a steady, staggered renewal | FY24 Annual Report, p.13, p.17; `01_management-and-track-record.md` §2 |

Every related-party category above sits far under the module's Low-materiality threshold (<1% of revenue/assets), and Note 10 names every counterparty individually (Zone Elite Investment LLC, Toku Pte. Ltd., BIO-LUTIONS Eco Tech India, and each Management/Supervisory Board member's outside occupation). The Audit Committee holds explicit approval authority over "material transactions between the Company... and a member of the Management Board or a related party" and over related-party transactions under Section 111b(1) AktG [FY24 Annual Report, p.29]. **Read: economic value is not leaking to a promoter or founder group here** — Delivery Hero has no controlling promoter, and the RPT footprint is immaterial and individually disclosed. `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` independently confirms RPT is nowhere near the >25%-of-revenue/expenses disqualifier threshold (computed at ≈0.2%). No RF-RPT-001/002/003 trigger.

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | No | No shareholder-rights plan or blank-check preferred structure is disclosed; German law does not generally permit US-style poison pills, and the Management Board's authority to issue shares against a hostile bid is legally constrained (WpÜG Section 33 board-neutrality rule) absent a specific prior AGM authorization, none of which is disclosed here | FY24 Annual Report, p.32–39 (Takeover-related Disclosures) |
| Classified / staggered board | No | The terms of all 8 Supervisory Board members ended together at the June 19, 2024 AGM and were re-elected/replaced as a single slate (staggered terms of 3–4 years then follow from individual appointment dates, but the board is not divided into rotating classes that prevent a full refresh) | FY24 Annual Report, p.17 |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No | "There are no different share classes... The same rights and obligations are associated with all shares. Each share grants one vote... There are no shares with special rights conferring powers of control" | FY24 Annual Report, p.33 |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | Majority (of votes cast) | Articles of Association Section 20(2): resolutions require a majority of valid votes cast (two-thirds for Articles-of-Association amendments), consistent with the general German AktG standard for Supervisory Board elections — not a US-style plurality regime that can seat an unopposed nominee with a single "for" vote | FY24 Annual Report, p.33 |
| Shareholder ability to call meetings / act by written consent | Not disclosed in this pool | The Annual Report does not reproduce the Articles of Association's provision (if any) on shareholders' statutory right to request an Extraordinary General Meeting. *Inference, not from filings:* German stock-corporation law (AktG Section 122) generally gives shareholders holding 5% of share capital the right to request the Supervisory Board convene a meeting or add agenda items, and out-of-meeting written-consent action is not a feature of German company law (all resolutions require a general meeting, in person or virtual under Section 118a AktG). This is general legal background, not a company-specific filing fact, and is flagged as **Insufficient Data** at the company-specific level | Not in pool — general AktG framework, labeled inference |
| Change-of-control provisions (deal-relevant given the live Uber tender) | Yes | On a change of control, Management Board members may resign within 3 months on 3 months' notice, with **all outstanding RSUs, PSUs, and stock options becoming fully and immediately vested** regardless of vesting schedules; convertible bonds carry change-of-control conversion-price adjustments; the €1.4bn-equivalent syndicated credit facility gives lenders the right to accelerate repayment on a change of control; certain software-license contracts terminate or become terminable if a direct competitor acquires the Company; the employee share-purchase plan's matching shares vest immediately on a change of control | FY24 Annual Report, p.32–33, p.39–40 (Compensation Report §3) |

**Read on takeover defenses.** Formally, DHER has almost no entrenchment machinery — single-class voting, no poison pill, majority (not plurality) director voting, and a Management Board that (per the WpÜG neutrality rule and the absence of any disclosed defensive-issuance authorization) has limited legal room to block a bid unilaterally. The live Uber process is proceeding without disclosed board resistance. The one real minority-relevant feature is the change-of-control equity acceleration: it gives the Management Board a personal financial incentive to see the Uber deal complete on favorable terms to itself, which is a conflict of interest to weigh against management's public support for the transaction, though not evidence of entrenchment.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| AGM votes against (remuneration / RPT / director / auditor resolutions) | Only one full resolution result is reproduced in this pool: the 2024 AGM (June 19, 2024) approved the **Compensation Report 2023 by 93.05%** (a say-on-pay advisory vote, audited by KPMG) — roughly 7% opposed/abstained, well under the module's >20% high-opposition threshold. No table of results for the other 2024 AGM resolutions (director elections, auditor appointment, discharge of Management/Supervisory Board) is in the pool | FY24 Annual Report, p.41 |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations (ISS / Glass Lewis / IiAS) | Not disclosed anywhere in the pool | Insufficient Data |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | Not evidenced beyond the single 93.05% figure above; no breakdown by holder | Insufficient Data |
| Dilution history (preferential allotment, warrants, QIP, ESOP pool) | Material and real: shares outstanding rose from **199.3m (Dec 31, 2020) to 298.2m (Dec 31, 2025) — a ~49.6% increase over five years**, driven by convertible-bond conversions, LTIP/PSU/RSU settlements, and in-kind capital increases tied to the Woowa Brothers and Glovo NCI buyouts, against **zero DHER-level share buybacks** in the same period. The Company maintains an unusually large stack of parallel Authorized and Conditional Capital pools (at least nine named pools active or partially utilized as of Dec 31, 2024 — Authorized Capital 2020/I, 2020/II, 2021, 2022/I, 2023/I, 2023/II, 2023/III, 2024/I, 2024/II, plus Conditional Capital 2017/II, 2019/I, 2020/I, 2021/I, 2023/I, 2023/II), giving management substantial standing headroom to issue new shares (subscription rights excludable only for defined purposes — LTIP grants and M&A consideration — with Supervisory Board consent, not a general-purpose defensive tool) | Cross-referenced from `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` §1 (share-count trajectory, severity 58/100 inverted); FY24 Annual Report, p.33–38 (Authorized/Conditional Capital detail) |

High AGM opposition is not evidenced here (93.05% approval is clean), so **RF-SHR-001 is not triggered**. The dilution pattern is disclosed, earmarked (not used for entrenchment), and already scored by the capital-allocation module — it is not "controversial" in the RF-SHR-002 sense of a preferential allotment used to dodge shareholder approval, so **RF-SHR-002 is not triggered** here either, but the ~50% five-year dilution with no offsetting buyback is a real minority-shareholder cost (each existing share's claim on the Company was cut by roughly a third over five years) and is scored Amber below.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

On paper, the architecture is genuinely clean: one share, one vote, no dual class, no poison pill, majority (not plurality) director elections, immaterial related-party leakage (≈0.2% of revenue, individually disclosed and Audit-Committee-approved), and a functioning four-committee Supervisory Board that documents its work in detail. Set against that: roughly half the Supervisory Board turned over in a single year (2024), the FY2024 Audit Committee chair was the operating partner of the Company's sole >10% reference shareholder while self-assessed "independent," shares outstanding grew ~50% in five years against zero buybacks, and the AGM is virtual-only, which narrows real-time shareholder engagement even though it is legal under Section 118a AktG. None of this amounts to classic entrenchment — management is not blocking a bid, has not adopted defensive share issuance, and is publicly cooperating with the Uber process. The single biggest risk to minority holders is forward-looking, not historical: Uber's own stated plan to "evaluate the most appropriate ownership structure" once its economic ownership (with Prosus/Naspers's irrevocably tendered stake) crosses 50% raises the live possibility of a post-closing squeeze-out (Section 327a AktG, a 95% ownership threshold) or a domination agreement (a ~75% AGM threshold) that would set the terms on which any remaining minority stake is compressed or cashed out — a process where a newly dominant Uber, not today's dispersed shareholder base, would control the outcome. Combined with a Management Board whose equity fully vests on a change of control (a real, if modest, conflict of interest in supporting the deal), the verdict is **adequate**: today's formal rights are intact and better than a US dual-class or promoter-controlled peer, but the protection minority holders actually get from here depends heavily on how the pending change-of-control process plays out, not on anything the board can now guarantee. Separately, and independent of this board-quality read, the auditor-flagged Glovo Spain going-concern note (`business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`) caps the Group's overall stewardship verdict at no better than "Serious governance concerns" downstream, regardless of the board architecture assessed here.

## Board & Shareholder Rights Score

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Board independence | 13 | 20 | German codetermination structure (4 of 8 seats are employee representatives, not assessed for independence) limits the base; among the 4 shareholder seats, the ">half independent" target is asserted but individually thin in the extracted text (05-001–05-003) |
| True independence / tenure / overboarding | 11 | 20 | Heavy FY2024 board churn (5 of 8 seats, Red) and a lapsed but real Audit-Committee-chair/reference-shareholder overlap (05-004) offset by clean current-director overboarding and reasonable term/age limits (05-006–05-009) |
| Committee quality | 12 | 15 | Four functioning committees (Audit, Remuneration, Nomination, Strategy) with documented meeting cadence, financial-expertise compliance, and near-universal attendance (05-005, 05-011) |
| RPT and group-leakage protection | 17 | 20 | RPT is immaterial (≈0.2% of revenue), individually disclosed by counterparty, and subject to real Audit Committee approval authority under Section 111b AktG (05-010–05-012) |
| Shareholder voting rights | 9 | 15 | One-share-one-vote and no poison pill are genuinely protective (05-013–05-015), but heavy five-year dilution (~50%, no buyback offset) and virtual-only AGM format weigh against it (05-020) |
| AGM opposition / minority protection | 5 | 10 | The single available data point (93.05% say-on-pay approval) is clean, but AGM-voting disclosure breadth in this pool is thin (only 1 of an unknown number of resolutions), and the live Uber tender's squeeze-out/domination-agreement risk is the dominant forward concern (05-018–05-021) |
| **Raw Total** | **67** | **100** | Sum of components above |
| **Final Total (after partial-data cap)** | **60** | **100** | **Capped at 60/100 per MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules — "No ownership / insider-transaction data": the pool lacks a full beneficial-ownership table and a PDMR buy-sell transaction log (only the sole >10% WpHG notification is reproduced), per `00_governance-data-triage.md` §6.** |

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — DHER

**Data basis.** Two verbatim S&P Global Market Intelligence earnings-call transcripts are in the pool — the FY2025 full-year results call (Mar 26, 2026, CEO Niklas Oestberg and CFO Marie-Anne Popp, full prepared remarks plus a 9-question Q&A) and the Q1 2026 Sales/Trading Statement Call (Apr 30, 2026, same speakers). Both are genuine speaker-attributed transcripts with analyst names and firms, not paraphrases or press releases — so tone, Q&A directness, and "owning the miss" ARE assessable here, unlike a partial-data case. No prior shareholder letter exists under German AktG practice; the Management Board's letter inside the FY2024 Annual Report substitutes, per `01_management-and-track-record.md`. No FY2025 audited Annual Report is in the pool, so the newest written (non-transcript) disclosure available is ~15.6 months old (FY2024 Annual Report, published Apr 25, 2025).

## 1. Owning the Misses

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| FY2025 Free Cash Flow: -€246m normalized (CFO − total capex) vs. FY2024's +€358.8m, and a ~€366m miss against the ~€120m point guide on a reported basis | CFO led prepared remarks with "we have delivered positive free cash flow for a second year in a row" and "free cash flow growing 15% year-over-year to EUR 250 million" — both true only on the company's own post-Jan-2025 definition that excludes "extraordinary outflows" (the EU antitrust settlement payment and Glovo Spain rider-transition costs). When directly pressed by Andrew Ross (Barclays) on "true free cash flow... including cash exceptionals," Popp answered directly: "we guided on free cash flow, excluding extraordinary outflows... at this stage, the guidance we can give is on... cash flow before extraordinary items" — a candid, non-evasive answer to a pointed question, but the prepared-remarks framing never surfaced the ~€600–650m normalized-vs-guided gap `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` derives (Section 5 there) | **Mixed** — direct and specific once challenged in Q&A, but the unprompted headline framing ("second year in a row," "+15%") is built on a definitional exclusion the prepared remarks do not flag as a exclusion, and the practical effect (turning a genuinely negative operating FCF year into a reported "beat") recurs into FY2026 guidance on the same basis | FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, p.5 (prepared remarks), p.7 (Q&A, Andrew Ross exchange); `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` §5, §10 |
| FY2025 statutory net loss of -€782.9m (vs. -€882.4m FY2024) | CFO's ENTIRE verbal disclosure of the net-income line in prepared remarks was: "The net result improved by EUR 183 million." No absolute loss figure (-€782.9m) was spoken on the call; the improvement delta was given, the base was not | **Obscured by omission** — the delta is accurate but the magnitude of the underlying loss (still a €783m annual loss, the fifth of the last five years by the Street-EPS-miss record in `01`) is never stated aloud; a listener relying only on the call would not know the company posted a three-quarters-of-a-billion-euro net loss | FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, p.5 (prepared remarks) |
| CFO departure (Thomassin, Jun 2024) and 7-month partial Management Board vacancy | No mention in either transcript in the pool (both transcripts post-date the vacancy and the Popp appointment, so the topic never arose as current news); the only disclosure found is the matter-of-fact filing note itself | **Not obscured, but not proactively owned on a call either** — a plain, unemotional filing disclosure (Note 10, related-party section) rather than any spun or evasive framing, but also not a topic management chose to address candidly in front of analysts because it had already resolved by the time of the transcripts in the pool | FY24 Annual Report, Note 10(a)-(b), p.206–207 |
| "Path to profitability" / turnaround narrative resting on 3 years of Adjusted EBITDA hits vs. only 1 year of GAAP operating profit (per `01`'s Filter 2 finding) | CEO's opening framing: "2025 was a defining year for Delivery Hero. We came into the year with a clear plan, delivering profitable growth and provide -- or prove that our platform can evolve well beyond food delivery... I'm proud to say that we did both." The entire "profitable" claim in this framing traces to Adjusted EBITDA (+30% YoY, €903m) — GAAP operating profit (€93.7m, a fragile first year per `business-model/07_business-quality.md`) is never mentioned in prepared remarks | **Obscured by selective framing** — a real Adjusted EBITDA achievement is presented as unqualified "profitable growth" without naming the metric or noting it is the company's own non-GAAP definition, and without contrasting it against the much thinner GAAP operating result | FY2025 Earnings Call transcript, Mar 26 2026, p.4 (prepared remarks) |
| Five consecutive fiscal years (FY2021–FY2025) missing Street's EPS Normalized estimate | Not a company-set guidance metric — DHER does not guide EPS, so there is no promise to "own." Neither transcript addresses the EPS miss pattern at all; it is absent from both prepared remarks and Q&A in the pool | **Not applicable / silence** — management is not obligated to discuss a metric it does not guide, but the total absence of any framing (even a passing acknowledgment) leaves the five-year miss streak entirely unaddressed in the company's own words | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6; no transcript reference found |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Goodwill impairment recorded in every one of the last 5 fiscal years (2021–2025: -€85.9m / -€760.9m / -€857.8m / -€89.7m / -€259.7m), never zero; "Asset Writedown & Restructuring Costs" present in every one of the last 6 years (2020–2025), exceeding €700m in two of those years; stock-based compensation (€224.1m in FY2025, 24.8% of Adjusted EBITDA, up 31% YoY) excluded from the headline profitability metric every year — none of these meets a genuine one-off test given their unbroken recurrence | earnings/06 §4–5, §8 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | FY2025: reported (GAAP) EBITDA €304.9m vs. Adjusted EBITDA €903.0m — the €598.1m adjustment is 66% the size of the adjusted figure itself. FY2024: reported EBITDA -€24.3m vs. Adjusted €692.5m — the €716.8m adjustment EXCEEDS the entire adjusted figure. Both years are far above the 15%-of-GAAP-earnings threshold that should trigger scrutiny, and the gap is not narrowing | earnings/06 §4, §7 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Yes, materially. The FY2025 "second consecutive year of positive free cash flow" claim depends on excluding an implied ~€600–650m legal/regulatory cash outflow (`earnings/06` §5) that was genuinely spent in cash during the year on unresolved, still-live litigation (EU antitrust, Glovo Spain rider reclassification); the same exclusion methodology carries into the FY2026 ">€200m" guide for a company that discloses the same litigation as ongoing in at least three jurisdictions. The Earnings Quality module scored this a 36/100 ("Poor quality — significant gap between reported earnings and cash") | earnings/06 §5, §9, §10 |

**RF-DISC-002 triggered.** The goodwill-impairment-every-year, restructuring-costs-every-year, and SBC-excluded-every-year pattern is a recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-back pattern by definition — five straight years for two of the three items, six straight years for the third.

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Management discloses granular operating KPIs (GMV like-for-like growth, own-delivery mix shift 67%→78%, subscriber penetration 43–61% of GMV by market, AdTech revenue run-rate) with real specificity, and the company's SASB disclosure of 8 data-breach incidents in FY2024 (all involving personal data, 35,343 users affected) is a genuine, non-obvious transparency point most companies would omit (`business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`). Set against that: segment-level profit is disclosed only on the heavily adjusted Adjusted EBITDA basis with a €683.0m unallocated bridge back to the audited Group operating result (`business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`, segment-map finding), and the Free Cash Flow definition itself was changed effective Jan 1, 2025 to newly exclude "extraordinary outflows" — a genuine reduction in comparability adopted in the same year those exclusions first did large (~€600–650m) work, which is the single disclosure change in this record that got LESS transparent over time rather than more.

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

The worst recent period by the numbers is FY2025: a -€782.9m statutory net loss, cash conversion (CFO/Adjusted EBITDA) collapsing to 8.8%, and a headline FCF outcome that is negative once normalized. The CEO's opening framing of that year was unreservedly upbeat — "2025 was a defining year," "I'm proud to say that we did both" — built entirely around the Adjusted EBITDA growth story, with the statutory net loss's absolute size never spoken aloud (only the €183m year-over-year improvement was verbalized) and GAAP operating profit not mentioned in prepared remarks at all. Q&A was mostly direct: Popp gave a specific, non-evasive answer on the FCF exclusion methodology and on the €520–860m Glovo Spain contingent-liability range when pressed by Barclays and Citi analysts respectively, and Oestberg answered a pointed question about the Uber "process overhang" plainly ("it's obviously a clear problem for us... it's not our decision"). One partial exception: pressed on a 40% Korean order-growth figure, Oestberg first deflected ("we don't want to comment on potential external data... it's pretty random") until the analyst pointed out the number was from the company's OWN press release, at which point he downplayed his own disclosed figure ("I wouldn't draw too much around that") — a minor but real instance of distancing from a number the company itself had published.

## 5. Candor Read

Delivery Hero's management answers direct, pointed analyst questions candidly and with real specificity — the FCF-exclusion mechanics, the Spain contingent-liability math, and the Uber-overhang admission were all handled without evasion when an analyst pushed. Set against that: the unprompted, prepared-remarks framing consistently leads with the flattering non-GAAP number (Adjusted EBITDA "profitable growth," "second consecutive year of positive free cash flow") while the absolute size of the statutory net loss is never spoken and the GAAP operating result goes unmentioned — a pattern of selective emphasis rather than fabrication, but selective emphasis all the same, compounded by adjustments that recur every year rather than being genuinely one-off (Section 2) and by a FCF-definition change adopted in the same year the exclusion first mattered most. The single biggest candor signal is the gap between the two registers: management is honest under cross-examination but consistently chooses the more flatteringframing when it controls the microphone — a pattern this report classifies as **"mixed"** rather than "candid / high-trust" or "promotional / low-trust." Per CLAUDE.md §24 Filter 1 / §13, no unresolved adverse personal-integrity signal was routed from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` to weigh against this (`01_management-and-track-record.md` §4A confirms none found) — but the Glovo Spain going-concern disqualifier itself (a subsidiary-level auditor emphasis-of-matter, not a candor finding on its own) sits alongside this mixed communication record rather than being netted out against it, consistent with the instruction that a promotional tone alongside unresolved integrity/going-concern questions compounds rather than offsets.

RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)
RF-DISC-001 (commentary contradicting the numbers)
